### Layout and placement (rule 40)

| Requirement | Ohio | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Type size / point type in a lease | **None** | `"point type"` 29 hits — ch. 1345, 1349, 1351 only; R.C. 1351.02 is lease-**purchase** of goods, not realty |
| Boldface / bold type in a lease | **None** | `boldface` 65, `"bold type"` 5 — nothing in ch. 5321 or 1923. **Trap:** R.C. 4781.51 has one, for manufactured-home parks |
| Underlining | **None** | `underlined` 9 — nothing landlord-tenant; R.C. 5322.06 is self-storage |
| Capital letters | **None** | `"capital letters"` 22 — ch. 1345 CSPA only, which does not reach a lease of realty |
| Conspicuousness | **Yes, but for a NOTICE** | R.C. 1923.04(A): the prescribed language "printed or written in a conspicuous manner" — read section-open |
| Separate document / writing / instrument | **None** | 6 / 2 / 16 hits, none in ch. 5321 |
| Mandated lease content | **Two** | R.C. 5321.18 (owner and agent identity, every written agreement); R.C. 5321.031 (college or university letting to a student tenant) |
| Omission sanction forfeiting money | **One, indirect** | R.C. 5321.18(C): failure waives the landlord's right to receive the tenant's 5321.07 and 5321.08 notices — the tenant may go straight to escrow |

No two rules claim the same place, so there is nothing for Taylor to settle on ordering.

### Targeted fixes

| # | Item | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| 22 | **Rule 62 vetting — `default-by-tenant-ks-ne`** (KS, NE, OH, OK). NE proposes appending: *"Landlord need not give Tenant an opportunity to cure any breach, including a failure to pay Rent, where applicable law permits Landlord to proceed without one."* | **Lawful and accurate in Ohio. Support.** The sentence is self-limiting, so it cannot reduce a protection Ohio gives. Ohio's three tracks, all read section-open: **nonpayment** — no statutory cure right at all, because R.C. 1923.04 is a notice to *leave the premises*, not a pay-or-quit notice, so "applicable law permits Landlord to proceed without one" is simply true; **a R.C. 5321.05 breach materially affecting health and safety** — R.C. 5321.11 requires not less than **30 days**, and R.C. 1923.02(A)(8) makes compliance a condition precedent to filing, so the sentence correctly does *not* apply; **drug activity** under R.C. 5321.05(A)(9) — R.C. 5321.17(C) is a mandatory **3-day** no-cure termination. The sentence also fixes a real Ohio indeterminacy: the rent limb currently conditions default on failure to cure "within the time period specified by applicable law", and in Ohio there is no such period. Shared text not edited. |
| 23 | **Scrub-trimmed clauses** — `termination-notice-oh` | **No restoration needed.** Both figures are **floors** ("at least 30", "at least 7"), so no lawful-looking landlord figure can go under the statute; **R.C. 5321.17 requires no writing**, checked specifically, so the clause's silence on form breaks nothing; and the (D) breach exclusion with its express R.C. 5321.05(A)(9) exception **is** carried by `edu-termination-notice-periods-oh`, which was read for this purpose. On the second limb — text a statute requires in the lease itself — **Ohio's required-content provisions were searched, not just each clause's own citation**: R.C. 5321.18 (carried by `landlord-identity-oh`, still active) and the newly found R.C. 5321.031. Neither belongs in `termination-notice-oh`. `security-deposit-return-oh`, also trimmed, keeps the forwarding-address duty and its consequence, and R.C. 5321.16 requires no lease text. |
| 24 | **Scrub-switched-off required clauses** | **No restoration needed for any of them; one produced a rule 54 question instead.** `security-deposit-interest-oh` — R.C. 5321.16(A) imposes a duty to **compute and pay**, not to disclose; no statute requires the term in the lease. Education is the right home. `landlord-maintenance-oh` — R.C. 5321.04 imposes duties and R.C. 5321.13(E) **prohibits contrary terms**; neither requires affirmative lease text. (I argued for this clause on 2026-09-27 and Taylor reversed it on 2026-09-29; the retro question is the narrower one — is it a *required notice*? — and the answer is no.) `fire-casualty-termination-oh` — R.C. 5301.11 is a **default rule the lease may contract around**, not a required notice; switching the clause off leaves §5301.11's tenant-favourable default in place, which is the lawful outcome, and the landlord-side option it exposes is row 17(b), flagged for Taylor. Also checked though outside the prompt's list: `flag-display-oh` — R.C. 5321.131 is a **prohibition** on lease restrictions, not a required lease statement; no restoration. |
| 25 | **Rule 62 vetting — `returned-payments`.** AZ proposes "during any 12-month period" for "during the Term". | **Lawful in Ohio, and an improvement. Support.** No Ohio statute speaks to returned-payment counts or to requiring certified funds — confirmed by code-wide search (`"late fee"` 21 hits, none residential-rental; R.C. 1319.16 is a collection-agency cap that 1319.12(A)(2) excludes a landlord from). R.C. 5321.14 unconscionability is the only ceiling, and a 12-month window is easier to defend than one tied to a Term. It also fixes a rule 37 defect for Ohio specifically: R.C. 5321.17 recognises week-to-week and month-to-month tenancies that **have no Term**, so "during the Term" has no referent for them. Shared text not edited. |
| 26 | **Rule 62 vetting — `early-termination`.** WY proposes making the landlord limb subordinate: "…or such shorter notice and cure period as applicable law permits". | **Lawful in Ohio, and Ohio needs it more than Wyoming does. Support.** The current limb promises 30 days' notice plus a 10-day cure. It gives up a shorter statutory route in **two** Ohio cases. (a) A **written-lease breach**: R.C. 1923.02(A)(9) carries no R.C. 5321.11 requirement, so the statutory route is the R.C. 1923.04 three-day notice to leave with **no cure period at all**. (b) **Drug activity**: R.C. 5321.17(C) requires a **three-day** notice and R.C. 5321.04(A)(9) makes termination and prompt action **mandatory** — so a 30-day contractual promise does not merely waste time, it conflicts with a duty the statute says the landlord "shall" perform. Both sections read section-open. The proposed wording is self-limiting and reduces no tenant protection. **Also flagged on the same clause (rule 37, not WY's point):** the tenant limb's fee of "one month's Rent or 30% of the remaining Rent due under the Term, whichever is greater" has no referent in a periodic tenancy, where the greater-of collapses to one month's rent. That is a separate shared-text question for whoever consolidates; Ohio has no statute on early-termination fees, so it is lawful either way here, with R.C. 5321.14 the only ceiling. Shared text not edited. |

### Legal watch

**Add: R.C. ch. 3780 → ch. 3796, renumbered by S.B. 56, 136th G.A., effective 2026-03-20.** Verified from the heading of R.C. 3796.34, which still reads "[Former R.C. 3780.37, amended and renumbered by S.B. 56 …]". This is the **second** instance of the same renumbering wave on Ohio's watch list, after R.C. 955.011 → 955.021 by H.B. 247, also effective 2026-03-20. No library row cited 3780, so nothing was left dangling — but Ohio guidance published before March 2026 still cites it.

**Add: Ohio Rules of Superintendence restructuring, effective 2026-07-01.** Former Sup.R. 44–47 (public access to court records) have been renumbered and the mapping was not resolved this pass. Open item, carried from rule 39.

### Proposed SOP changes

1. **Rule 79 — add the scrub-successor case explicitly to the counting instruction.** Rule 78 already says "a moved row whose notes record no basis drops out of rule 79's screen", and rule 79 says to report the no-basis count. Ohio shows the two interacting in a way neither quite names: **all five rows the 2026-09-29 scrub created or renamed recorded provenance and a citation but no basis**, so they appeared in the no-basis count as if never verified, three days after a pass that did not re-verify anything. Suggested addition to rule 79: *"Report the no-basis count split between rows a research pass wrote and rows a library-wide pass created or renamed; the second group is a provenance repair, not a re-verification, and is fixed by carrying the source row's basis forward."* That keeps the count honest without inflating what looks like unverified work.

2. **Rule 40 — add the sibling-chapter trap to the battery instruction.** Rule 40 already says to run the battery across the whole code. Ohio's result shows the specific way a false positive arrives: the only Ohio statutes with boldface, type-size and underlining rules that *look* like lease-content rules are **R.C. 4781.51** (manufactured-home parks, "Information to be contained in rental agreement" — a heading that reads exactly like a residential lease rule) and **R.C. 5322.06** (self-service storage). Suggested addition: *"Check each formatting hit's chapter before recording it: a manufactured-home-park, self-storage or lease-purchase chapter will produce rules whose headings read like residential lease-content rules and are not."* Ohio has now hit the park-chapter trap three times (R.C. 1923.12–.14 abandoned property, R.C. 1923.04(C) deceased residents, R.C. 4781.51).

3. **Rule 27 — say that a topic's answer may be a clause, not only education.** Rule 27 says the new state needs a row "as a clause or as education (including a confirmed absence)", and in practice the seven listed topics have produced education rows almost everywhere. Ohio's `cannabis` answer is a **clause**, because R.C. 3796.04(A)(1)(d)(iii) makes the lease the operative instrument and an education row would leave the landlord with nothing to use. Suggested addition: *"Where the statute conditions a right on what the rental agreement says, the topic's answer is a clause and an education row alone does not discharge it (rule 50, rule 54)."*

### Fix 1 — `casualty-landlord-termination-oh`

**What R.C. 5301.11 actually says**, read section-open and quoted in full because every limit in the clause is keyed to it:

> "The lessee of a building which, without fault or neglect on his part, is destroyed or so injured as to be unfit for occupancy, is not liable to pay rent to the lessor or owner thereof, after such destruction or injury, unless otherwise expressly provided by written agreement or covenant. The lessee thereupon must surrender possession of such premises."

**Why the lease is the only source of a landlord right.** R.C. 5301.11 gives the *tenant* rent relief and imposes a surrender duty, and says nothing about the landlord ending the lease. R.C. 5321.06 lets a rental agreement carry any term not inconsistent with ch. 5321 or any other rule of law, so a landlord termination right is contractual or it does not exist. **The gap it fills is specific:** under the statutory default alone, rent stops and the tenant surrenders, but a fixed Term keeps running — so the tenant may claim a right to re-occupy once repairs are done. That is the hole, and it is the reason the clause is worth having rather than a generic add-on.

**The three limits the sync required, and how each is met.**

1. **R.C. 5301.11 left intact.** The clause does not use the statute's "unless otherwise expressly provided by written agreement or covenant" hook at all. Its penultimate sentence preserves both limbs of the tenant's right — no rent after the destruction or injury, and surrender — in the statute's own terms.
2. **Rent stops at the unfit date**, not at the notice date and not at termination. That tracks the statute's "after such destruction or injury" rather than improving on it. Pre-casualty arrears stay payable; post-casualty prepaid rent is refunded.
3. **R.C. 5321.13(E) is not engaged.** No rent runs after the property becomes unfit, so there is no receipt of rent free of the R.C. 5321.04 obligation. The clause also expressly preserves Landlord's obligations owed while the Lease was in force and every tenant remedy for a pre-termination failure to meet them, so the R.C. 5321.07 escrow and damages routes survive termination.

**Naming follows Kansas; the body follows Michigan.** The id is `casualty-landlord-termination-oh` because Ohio's structure is Kansas's: the tenant-side `fire-casualty-termination-ks` and `fire-casualty-termination-oh` are both switched off and the live clause is the landlord-side one (same in AZ and AL). The **text** is modelled on `casualty-termination-mi` and `casualty-termination-ia` as the prompt directs, and MI is the right model because **Mich. Comp. Laws § 554.201 is a near-twin of R.C. 5301.11** — an old-style surrender-and-no-rent provision — whereas KS, AZ, AL and IA are URLTA-style substantial-impairment-plus-rent-reduction states. Both models read; neither tagged.

**Two deliberate departures from the models, to avoid importing another state's architecture (rule 19).**
- **MI limits its clause to a Term of at least one year. Ohio gets no year.** R.C. 5301.11 has no term-length condition anywhere, so importing "one year" would invent a threshold Ohio law does not have. The clause is limited to a **fixed Term** instead, on an Ohio-specific ground: for a periodic tenancy the landlord already has a no-cause exit under R.C. 5321.17(A) and (B) (7 and 30 days), so a casualty clause would add nothing and only create confusion.
- **IA's 30-day notice window and 14-day minimum effective date are not imported.** Ohio has no statutory casualty notice period, and for a fixed Term R.C. 5321.17 does not apply at all — (A) and (B) reach only week-to-week and month-to-month tenancies. The clause uses a landlord-set repair window and a notice effective on the date stated, with the KS/AZ fallback to the date the tenant actually surrendered.

"Landlord may recover possession only as Ohio law permits" is there for **R.C. 5321.15(A)**: a termination right is not a self-help right. Optional and never a default — `rule_type` CONDITIONAL, `is_default` blank, body opens "[Optional.]", matching `casualty-landlord-termination-ks` and `-az`.

**`edu-casualty-and-mitigation-waivable-oh` changed to match.** Two sentences added: one pointing at the new clause and naming the re-occupancy gap it closes, one recording **what the library declines to offer and why** — a term keeping rent running after the home becomes unfit. R.C. 5301.11 appears on its face to permit that, but R.C. 5321.13(E) bars any agreement letting a landlord receive rent free of the R.C. 5321.04 duties and R.C. 5321.04 cannot be waived, so it would collide with Chapter 5321 even though R.C. 5301.11 sits outside it. This is the rule 54 verdict (a) I recorded on 2026-10-02 **in the log only**; rule 54 requires a declined lawful option to live in an education row, never the log, because landlords never see the log. Same pattern as `edu-casualty-mi`.

**Not researched:** whether any Ohio court has tested a landlord casualty-termination clause against R.C. 5321.14, and whether the *Lemstone* mitigation line bears on a casualty termination (*Lemstone* is a commercial holding). No case law read — flagged in the row.

---

### Fix 2 — Ohio moved to `early-termination-ks`

**Nothing in Ohio law points the other way**, and the variant fixes two Ohio problems, not one.

- **The fixed-Term limb answers the problem I raised.** "One month's Rent or 30% of the remaining Rent due under the Term, whichever is greater" has no referent in a month-to-month or week-to-week tenancy, where the greater-of silently collapses to one month's rent. The variant's sentence confines the option and fee to a fixed Term and sends a periodic tenancy to the notice the law and the lease provide — for Ohio, R.C. 5321.17(B)'s 30 days before the periodic rental date or (A)'s 7 days before the termination date stated in the notice, with no fee. Worth noting: **R.C. 5321.17 requires no writing**, so "written notice" in the clause is the lease's own requirement, not the statute's.
- **The landlord limb resolves the check-26 conflict more cleanly than the hedge did.** The parent now carries "or on such shorter notice and cure period as applicable law permits" (the WY fix). The variant instead defers to the Tenant Default and notice provisions — for Ohio, `default-by-tenant-ks-ne`, `notices` and `termination-notice-oh` — which removes the 30-day/10-day promise altogether. That matters because Ohio's statutory routes are shorter in two cases: a **written-lease breach**, where R.C. 1923.02(A)(9) carries no R.C. 5321.11 requirement and the route is the R.C. 1923.04 three-day notice to leave with no cure period; and **drug activity**, where R.C. 5321.17(C) requires a three-day notice and R.C. 5321.04(A)(9) makes prompt termination and action **mandatory**, so a contractual 30-day promise would conflict with a duty the statute says the landlord *shall* perform. R.C. 5321.11's 30-day cure still governs a R.C. 5321.05 health-and-safety breach and remains a condition precedent to filing under R.C. 1923.02(A)(8).

**Ohio's substantive findings were carried forward, not dropped.** `early-termination`'s OH segment held two things worth keeping, and both are now in `early-termination-ks`'s OH segment: no Ohio statute caps or bars an early-termination fee, with R.C. 5321.14 unconscionability the only ceiling and the greater-of-one-month-or-30% figure carrying real exposure under it; and Ohio's mitigation duty rests on *Frenchtown Square Partnership v. Lemstone*, a **commercial** holding, and is waivable because it sits outside ch. 5321 — so **never stack a mitigation waiver on top of this fee for an Ohio lease** (Taylor's decision 2026-09-18: no mitigation waiver in the default Ohio lease). The savings sentence's SCRA limb stays accurate for Ohio, which has no state servicemember residential termination right (`edu-no-servicemember-lease-rule-oh`); the tenant-death limb is a harmless hedge, Ohio having no statute on death of a tenant.

Same move as GA (2026-10-01), NC and NV (2026-10-02) and TX (2026-10-03). Both rows are shared, so only the OH tag, the OH note segment and `last_checked` were touched.

**One thing flagged rather than changed:** `early-termination-ks` is `REQUIRED` where the parent is `RECOMMENDED`. No Ohio statute requires an early-termination clause, so `REQUIRED` is the shared row's own product designation rather than a statement about Ohio law. Changing it would be a shared-text edit outside what this pass may touch, so it is noted in the row for the sync.

---

### Fix 3 — rule 62 vetting: `surrender-end-of-term`

**Is CA's qualified wording lawful and accurate in Ohio?** Yes, and I support it.

**Does the current wording state a duty Ohio law doesn't back?** **No** — and the premise behind the proposal does not hold in Ohio. Ohio has **no just-cause or good-cause rule** (`edu-for-cause-eviction-oh`, from the 2026-10-02 pass, with R.C. 5321.02, 5321.03, 5321.031 and 5321.17 all read section-open), so nothing gives an Ohio tenant a general right to stay past the term. Holding over is itself a ground for a possession action under R.C. 1923.02(A)(1), so the duty to surrender on expiration is backed. The Ohio limit falls on the landlord's **remedy**, not the tenant's duty: R.C. 5321.15(A) bars excluding a tenant outside a court process, which this clause does not purport to do.

**Where the qualification does bite in Ohio — two narrow cases, which is why it is still worth having.** R.C. 5321.02(B)(1) and (2) let a tenant subjected to retaliation use it as a defence to a possession action and even **recover possession**; and R.C. 1923.062 can **stay** an eviction against a deployed servicemember tenant (90 days unless justice requires otherwise, and not against a landlord operating fewer than four residential premises). In both, the tenant lawfully remains after the term and an unqualified "immediately" overstates.

The clause's third sentence is unchanged and was screened on 2026-10-02: "to the extent permitted by applicable law" resolves in Ohio to a near-zero disposal right, because the R.C. 1923.12–.14 machinery is manufactured-home-park only and R.C. 5321.15(B) bars seizing a tenant's possessions for rent except by court order. `edu-no-abandoned-property-safe-harbor-oh` carries that.

### Integrity

5 rows, 17 columns, header byte-identical to the master, CRLF throughout (6 CRLF, 0 bare LF). No duplicate ids; one new id absent from the master, four changed ids present in it. No blank `verification_status` (instruction 27) and no blank required field. No new `group` values and **no new `topic_key`** — the new clause reuses `casualty-termination`, which Ohio had no active row in after the scrub. No dangling `supersedes`. Field-by-field diffs against the master confirm the three shared rows changed only `states` and `notes` (`last_checked` on all three was already 2026-10-03, set today by the TX and CA passes, so setting it was a no-op). `early-termination` goes from 6 tagged states to 5 with OH removed; `early-termination-ks` from 20 to 21 with OH appended and no duplicates. `is_default` blank on the new clause. Citation format `R.C. x.xx` throughout my own text; two `§`-form citations remain inside **pre-existing** note segments on `early-termination` and `surrender-end-of-term`, left alone because rewriting another pass's text in a shared row is outside what this pass may change.

### Proposed SOP changes

1. **Rule 54 — say that "it would cause churn" is not a reason to defer an option.** This is the one the sync had to correct me on, so it is worth writing down rather than leaving to the next state to rediscover. My 2026-10-02 retro found the Ohio casualty option lawful, recorded it, and then withheld it because the three-bucket scrub had just switched Ohio's tenant-side casualty clause off three days earlier and adding a landlord-side clause in the same topic family felt like the churn rule 1 guards against. Rule 1 is about **re-auditing finished work**, not about adding a row a rule requires. Suggested addition to rule 54: *"Recent activity in a clause's topic family is not a reason to defer an option the rule requires. Rule 1 bars re-auditing finished work, not adding a row. Where a library-wide pass has just switched a related clause off, offer the option and say in the row how the two now fit together."*

2. **Rule 19 — add the near-twin-statute trap.** A prompt that names a model clause is a strong pull toward copying its figures, and the figures are exactly what does not travel. Ohio's model was MI because Mich. Comp. Laws § 554.201 and R.C. 5301.11 are structurally near-identical — which made MI's "Term of at least one year" limit look like part of the pattern, when Ohio's statute has no term-length condition at all. Suggested addition: *"When a prompt names a model clause, copy its structure and not its thresholds: a near-twin statute in another state is the reason to use the model and is not authority for any number in it. State for each figure in the new clause whether it comes from this state's statute, from the landlord, or from a drafting judgment, and name the ground."*

## Topic gaps (SOP 1.88) — Ohio

### §1 Sources, channel and currency

**Channel.** The Ohio corpus staged with the prompt (`corpus-OH-20261030.tar.xz`, archive SHA-256 `34eb224e...f152d70` verified against its `.sha256`; `verify_oh.py`: 974 files match, 200 listed but not shipped, 0 problems). It holds 37 Revised Code chapters as codes.ohio.gov chapter pages, **1,953 sections** (matching its README), each with its Effective and Latest Legislation lines; authenticated section PDFs for eight core chapters; the Constitution; Ohio Adm.Code 4112-1, -3, -5, -6, 109:4-3, 4781-12 and Fire Code 1301:7-7; and every act of the 135th and 136th General Assemblies.

A section-level index and search tool were built over the corpus text and validated in both directions before use (control term `zqxvbnmwt` returns 0 sections; `security deposit` returns 8). The section printer was **fixed** after it truncated R.C. 5321.16 at the authenticated-PDF link, and its output was cross-checked against that section's authenticated PDF text (SOP rule 80: a checker's printer must print the whole section).

**Out-of-corpus text.** codes.ohio.gov is unreachable from the working container this pass: `curl` is refused by the egress proxy and WebFetch answers `URL_NOT_ON_ALLOWLIST` for that host. The chapters and rules it does not hold were read in the built-in browser; the list follows.

Read in the built-in browser on **2026-10-30**, each whole, and recorded in the pass's `FETCHED_SECTIONS.md` which the canvass agents worked from — **49 sections and rules**: `3113.31`, `5311.19`, `5312.11`, `3796.04`, `3796.221`, `1335.04`, `1335.05`, `2305.06`, `2305.07`, `1343.03`, `4933.121`, `4933.12`, `1343.01`, `1343.02`, `1343.04`, `5703.47`, `1319.12`, `1319.16`, `4901:1-18-08`, `4705.01`, `1925.17`, `111.42`, `111.43`, `3749.01`, `4905.02`, `4905.03`, `4901:1-18-06`, `3742.41`, `3742.42`, `5311.26`, `5301.85`, `5301.88`, `1506.07`, `743.04`, `2711.01`, `5104.054`, `111.99`, `2953.26`, `5301.01`, `5301.08`, `5303`, `5301.63`, `5301.254`, `163.01`, `1311.02`, `5311.25`, `519`, `5739.01`, `1533.10`. Chapter 1335's and chapter 1343's chapter pages print only a section index, so those were fetched section by section. Two entries in that list are CHAPTERS rather than sections: ch. 5303 (ejectment) was read whole, and ch. 519 was read only as its section index, which is all the accessory-dwelling-unit question needed. One further page was read and produced no usable section - the smoke-and-CO groundwork note in that file, which records what the Residential Code rule would have carried had it been extractable. **Ohio Adm.Code 4101:8-3-01**, the Residential Code's building-planning rule that would carry the smoke-alarm and carbon-monoxide requirements, is published only as a PDF and yields no extractable text; its metadata was captured and its requirements could not be read.

**Currency.** Taken from the prompt and confirmed against the corpus: site current to 2026-10-22; Am. Sub. H.B. 33, 135th G.A. (R.C. 5321.01, 5321.03, 5321.051, 1923.02, eff. 2023-10-03); S.B. 237, 135th G.A. (R.C. 1923.01, 1923.05, eff. 2025-04-09); **H.B. 173, 136th G.A. vetoed 2026-06-25 and not law** — R.C. 5321.04's Latest Legislation line is still H.B. 490, 129th G.A., confirmed by reading the section. Every row records the Effective date and Latest Legislation line of each section it relies on.

**Two currency findings the corpus could not carry,** because it tracks only acts touching its 37 held chapters:

- **R.C. 2305.06 was amended by S.B. 219, 136th G.A., effective 2026-09-23** (site stamp 'Last updated July 6, 2026'). Chapter 2305 is not held, so this act is absent from `currency.json`.

- **R.C. ch. 3780 no longer exists.** It was amended and renumbered into **ch. 3796**, now titled *Marijuana Control Program*, by **S.B. 56, 136th G.A., effective 2026-03-20**; `chapter-3780` returns no chapter, and R.C. 3796.01 still cross-references the former '3780.28'. Ohio item 4 asks for the two as separate chapters; they are one. This is the second instance of the same 2026-03-20 renumbering wave already on Ohio's legal watch (R.C. 955.011 → 955.021, H.B. 247).


### §2 Topics answered

**The tally, since four numbers here are easy to misread.** 257 topics answered: **144** confirmed absences, **103** present, **6** answered by tagging an existing shared row alone, **4** already answered by a row the library had. Those verdicts describe OHIO LAW. The library actions are counted separately: **260 new rows** (247 primary plus 13 companion rows added under rule 57) and **8 existing rows tagged with OH** = **268 delta rows**, of which **37** are lease clauses and **231** education rows, and **254** are VERIFIED against **14** NEEDS_REVIEW. Two reconciliations worth stating. (1) Eight rows are tagged but only six topics have the verdict TAG, because `extended-absence-notice` and `owner-move-in-reservation` are both CONFIRMED_ABSENT on the law AND carry a tag: Ohio has no statute on either subject, so each got a confirmed-absence education row with its battery, and the existing shared clause was separately read in full and found lawful in Ohio under R.C. 5321.06, so it was tagged as written. The verdict answers 'what does Ohio law say'; the tag answers 'may this clause be used here'. (2) The delta has 253 rows keyed to topics rather than 257, because the four ALREADY_ANSWERED topics add no row at all.

### §4 Decisions, and questions asked of Taylor

## Ohio items

Items 3, 4, 5 and 7 are closed below. Item 1 is closing through the canvass
(`habitability-waiver` answered in B02; `smoke-drift-waiver`, `collection-fee` and
`eviction-penalty-clause-ban` sit in B10-prohibited and B04-fees-rent). Item 2 is in B07-dv-protected.
Item 6 is in B08-safety.

### Item 3 — shared fee clauses against R.C. 5321.13 and 5321.16

**`keys` — no conflict.** The clause's charge ("Landlord may re-key the applicable locks and charge
the cost to Tenant") is reached by nothing in R.C. 5321.13(A)–(F): it is not attorney's fees under
(C), not exculpation or indemnity of the landlord's own liability under (D), and it waives no chapter
provision under (A) — I read R.C. 5321.04's ten limbs and none gives a tenant any right to keys,
locks or re-keying, so there is no chapter right to waive. R.C. 5321.16(B) expressly authorises
applying the deposit to it: a re-key cost caused by the tenant's failure to return keys is squarely
'damages that the landlord has suffered by reason of the tenant's noncompliance with section 5321.05
of the Revised Code **or the rental agreement**'. Worth noting but not a conflict: the clause charges
"the cost" with no figure and Ohio caps nothing, so R.C. 5321.14 unconscionability is the only
ceiling — the same position as every other Ohio money term, which `edu-no-statutory-caps-oh` already
states.

**`hoa-compliance` — conflict; reported in §6 with the wording I would use.** Its first sentence is
better supported in Ohio than in most states, because R.C. 5311.19(A) puts the duty on the tenant
directly: 'All unit owners, **their tenants**, all persons lawfully in possession and control of any
part of a condominium property, and the unit owners association … shall comply with all covenants,
conditions, and restrictions set forth in a deed to which they are subject or in the declaration, the
bylaws, or the rules of the unit owners association.' The second sentence — "Any fines incurred due
to Tenant's violation of association rules will be Tenant's responsibility" — is the problem. Ohio
levies the charge on the **owner**: R.C. 5311.19(B)(3) says the costs of an enforcement eviction
'**shall be charged to the unit owner** and shall be the subject of a special assessment against the
offending unit and made a lien against that unit', and R.C. 5312.11(A) assesses **an individual lot**
for enforcement assessments and for costs incurred through the acts of 'an owner or occupant of a lot
or their family, **tenants**, guests, or invitees'. The association's claim is therefore a liability
of the **landlord**, arising under law and secured against the landlord's unit, and R.C. 5321.13(D)
says: 'No agreement by a tenant to the exculpation or limitation of any liability of the landlord
arising under law or to **indemnify the landlord for that liability or its related costs** shall be
recognized in any rental agreement or in any other agreement between a landlord and tenant.'

The fair counter-reading, and why this is a wording problem rather than a void clause: the same money
is recoverable as **damages for the tenant's breach of the lease**, and R.C. 5321.16(B) lets the
deposit be applied to damages from the tenant's noncompliance with the rental agreement. The lawful
route exists; the clause takes the indemnity route instead.

### Item 4 — the three central shared edits read correctly for Ohio

**`smoking-policy` — correct; no Ohio override needed.** Ohio legalised adult use by **initiated
statute**, not by constitutional amendment, so there is no Ohio counterpart to the Missouri
constitutional provision that forced `smoking-policy-mo`. R.C. 3796.221 (adult-use users' rights,
eff. 2026-03-20) frames its protection as freedom from arrest and prosecution and contains **no
'landlord' or 'rental agreement' sentence at all**. R.C. 3796.04 ('Home grow') then withholds the
home-grow right at '(iii) A residential premises occupied pursuant to a rental agreement that
prohibits the activities otherwise authorized by this section' — the General Assembly took lease
restriction for granted. A lease ban rests on R.C. 5321.06 and is lawful. What the clause does **not**
reach is cultivation: a smoking ban is not a prohibition of home grow, which is why Ohio needs the
separate `cannabis-cultivation-oh` it already has.

**`no-alterations` — correct.** Its savings sentence is accurate but, in Ohio, nearly empty: Ohio has
**no repair-and-deduct remedy** (R.C. 5321.07 gives rent escrow, a court order or termination, not
self-repair) and **no right to charge** for an EV installation (`edu-no-ev-charging-right-oh`). The
live limb is disability modification under R.C. 4112.02(H) and Ohio Adm.Code 4112-6. "Prior written
consent" collides with nothing in chapter 5321.

**`addendum-precedence` — correct, and it is what Ohio needs.** R.C. 5321.13(A) makes the whole
chapter non-waivable except through (F), so a lease term overriding a required disclosure would be
unrecognised anyway. Ohio's two mandated lease-content provisions are R.C. 5321.18 (owner and agent
identity) and R.C. 5321.031 (a college or university letting to its own student tenant); the
carve-out covers both.

### Item 5 — CARES Act: no Ohio row contradicts `edu-cares-act-notice`

I screened every OH-active row for a notice-period or filing statement. No contradiction: Ohio's rows
state the three-day notice as a **floor** — `edu-three-day-notice-language-oh` says "at least three
days before you file", not that three days is always enough — and `edu-tenant-cure-period-oh` already
tells landlords to give thirty days where the track is arguable.

One interaction worth flagging, which is **not** a contradiction: R.C. 5321.04(A)(9) makes a
drug-activity eviction mandatory — the landlord 'shall … **promptly commence** an action under Chapter
1923' after the R.C. 5321.17(C) three-day notice. On a property with a federally backed mortgage the
federal thirty-day wait and the state duty to proceed promptly pull against each other.
`edu-cares-act-notice` already records that courts disagree whether FEDERAL: 15 U.S.C. § 9058(c)
reaches only nonpayment, which is where the tension resolves. No row changed;
`edu-mandatory-drug-termination-oh` is not wrong as written.

### Item 7 — currency of the new rows

Confirmed as stated in §1. H.B. 173, 136th G.A. was **vetoed 2026-06-25 and is not law**; R.C.
5321.04's Latest Legislation line is still H.B. 490, 129th G.A., which I confirmed by reading the
section. One oddity already on Ohio's watch list and not re-opened: R.C. 5321.04(A)(10) cites the
Servicemembers Civil Relief Act as '117 Stat. 2835, 50 U.S.C. App. 501', a frozen-date citation to an
appendix since editorially reclassified.

## Decisions on points the canvass raised

Each of these came back from a canvass agent as a question. I settled them rather than hold them
(rule 76: work it out first); none needed Taylor.

**1. Four deposit rows left at NEEDS_REVIEW (`deposit-last-month-rent`, `fee-in-lieu-of-deposit`,
`nonrefundable-deposit-notice`, `holding-deposit`).** The agent asked whether to harden them to
VERIFIED on the statutory text alone. **Held at NEEDS_REVIEW, with the cautious course in each body.**
The core proposition is textual and strong — R.C. 5321.01(E)'s 'any deposit of money **or property**
to secure performance' plus R.C. 5321.13(A)'s non-waiver — but each of these four rows turns on how
that definition characterises a *specific commercial product*, which the text does not settle. Rule 1
makes case law a lead only, so a row can be neither verified by a case nor held pending one; the
cases the agent named (*Vardeman v. Llewellyn*, 17 Ohio St.3d 24 (1985), *Smith v. Padgett*, *Heritage
Hills v. Deacon*) stay unverified leads and nothing rests on them. NEEDS_REVIEW is the SOP's own
answer for a point the corpus cannot close.

**2. `deposit-cost-schedule` — no companion clause.** R.C. 5321.16(B)'s 'or the rental agreement'
limb is a genuine lease hook, but it hooks the **standard** of breach, not an amount, and rule 55's
CONSTRAINED_TERM needs the landlord's own figure. There is none here, the shared `tenant-maintenance`
row (which carries OH) already sets the condition standard, and a figure-free "turnover standards"
clause would duplicate it. Education only.

**3. `holding-deposit` — no standalone receipt form in this pass.** Pre-lease money cannot live in
the lease, so rule 55 yields no clause and the landlord is left with nothing usable. That is a real
gap, but a standalone form is a new artifact outside the seventeen-column CSV and outside this pass's
fence ("every answer is a row"). Carried to Proposed topic questions instead.

**4. `unpaid-damages-interest` — resolved by the lead, verdict changed.** The B01 agent saved
NOT_LOCATED because chapter 1343 is outside the corpus. I fetched R.C. 1343.01, 1343.02, 1343.03,
1343.04 and R.C. 5703.47 in the browser on 2026-10-30, read each whole, and rewrote the answer as
**PRESENT** with an education row (`edu-legal-interest-oh`) and a companion clause
(`unpaid-amounts-interest-oh`). Chapter 1343's chapter page prints only a section index, like chapter
1335, so sections were fetched individually. **No new variable:** the rate is a `[rate, not more than
8]%` bracketed prompt following the library's own convention in `unpaid-amounts-interest-id`, so the
agent's deferred `{{interest_rate_unpaid_amounts}}` is not needed. Per rule 19 the row states that the
eight per cent is **the statute's** ceiling (R.C. 1343.01(A)), the bracketed rate is **the
landlord's** figure, and the instruction to stay at or under eight per cent is **a drafting
judgment** — whether R.C. 1343.01(A) reaches a residential lease at all is unsettled on the text, and
R.C. 1343.04 would credit any excess against principal rather than void the debt.

This sets the rule for the rest of the pass: a `NOT_LOCATED` verdict from a canvass agent is a fact
about the agent's reach, not a finding about Ohio law, and the lead chases every one of them. Any
still open at the time of a delivery are listed in §2 with the chapter they need.

**5. `designated-repairer` — companion clause added (`designated-repairer-oh`).** The B02 agent asked
and recommended it; I agree and wrote it. Rule 54 is directly on point: recent activity in a clause's
topic family is not a reason to defer an option the rule requires. It is safe in Ohio precisely
*because* Ohio has no repair-and-deduct right for it to cut down. Minnesota's emergency-reimbursement
limb and its `{{emergency_response_hours}}` and `{{repair_reimbursement_days}}` variables were
deliberately **not** imported — they answer a Minnesota remedy Ohio does not have, which is rule 19's
near-twin-statute caution. `{{landlord_repair_contact}}` already exists and is reused.

**6. `maintenance-duty-shift` left at NEEDS_REVIEW — agreed.** A range and a dishwasher appear in both
R.C. 5321.04(A)(4) (landlord must maintain appliances the landlord supplied; non-waivable under
5321.13(A)) and R.C. 5321.05(A)(7) (a written lease may make the tenant maintain 'any range,
regrigerator, washer, dryer, dishwasher' — the misspelling is the statute's). The statutes do not
resolve the overlap. The agent drafted narrowly — tenant takes upkeep and misuse, landlord keeps
(A)(4) and all age-and-wear failures — and set NEEDS_REVIEW with that cautious course in the body.
Correct handling; kept. Note this is Ohio's **only** 'if the written rental agreement so provides'
maintenance hook, which is why the topic is a clause and not education (rule 27), and why the other
states' `maintenance-allocation-*` clauses must not be imported.

**7. `edu-habitability-waiver-oh` and `edu-one-way-delegation-oh` kept separate — agreed.** They quote
the same two divisions but answer different questions, and the framing file's instruction is to point
rather than restate. Each row's notes say how it divides from the other.

### Decisions on points B03-eviction and B04-fees-rent raised

**8. `entity-landlord-court-representation` — resolved by the lead, verdict changed.** The B03 agent
returned `NOT_LOCATED`, called it "the batch's likeliest real-world mistake" and asked me to
prioritise R.C. 4705.01 and 1925.17. It was right on every count. I fetched both in the browser on
2026-10-30 and rewrote the answer as **PRESENT** (`edu-entity-landlord-court-representation-oh`).
R.C. 4705.01 bars any person from "commence[ing], conduct[ing], or defend[ing] any action or
proceeding **in which the person is not a party concerned**" without bar admission — so a managing
agent cannot run the eviction, and R.C. 1923.01(C)(2)'s inclusion of the agent in "landlord" is a
definition for the eviction chapter, not a licence to practise for another. Ohio's only accommodation,
R.C. 1925.17, is confined to **a small claims division**, while **R.C. 1901.18(A)(8)** gives the
municipal court forcible entry and detainer as a separate grant — so it does not reach possession.
Two unsettled points are stated in the row rather than resolved: R.C. 1925.17 says "corporation", and
an LLC is not textually one; and R.C. 4705.01 excepts "rules adopted by the supreme court", which
were not reviewed. **No `NOT_LOCATED` verdict survives in the delivered delta.**

**9. `tax-escalation` — offer the clause as the agent drafted it.** The agent asked whether
R.C. 5321.13(D), which recognises no tenant agreement to indemnify the landlord for "any liability of
the landlord arising under law **or its related costs**", kills a tax-escalation clause, property tax
being exactly such a liability. It does not, and the reason is the same distinction I drew for
`hoa-compliance`: **(D) bars an indemnity; it does not bar pricing.** Ohio's own structure says to
recover a cost rise through rent — **R.C. 5321.02(C)** speaks of "increasing the rent … to reflect an
increase in other costs of operation", and **R.C. 5321.10(A)** lists "real estate taxes on the
premises" among the "customary and usual costs of operating the premises as a rental unit". A rent
adjustment measured by the tax rise leaves the landlord the taxpayer and the tenant owing rent;
nothing is indemnified. The agent's clause is already drafted that way, with an express
non-indemnity sentence and with penalties and interest from the landlord's own late payment excluded,
which is the right line. Kept at VERIFIED, and the agent was right to reject the Kentucky and Indiana
reimbursement framing under rule 26.

**10. `pet-fees` — companion clause added (`pet-deposit-oh`).** The agent noticed that the shared
`pet-policy` row, which carries OH, says "a pet deposit, if applicable" without saying that in Ohio a
refundable pet deposit **is** security-deposit money under R.C. 5321.01(E). That silence is the live
risk: it leads a landlord to hold the pet deposit as a separate or nonrefundable fund and so to miss
the R.C. 5321.16(B) thirty-day itemisation and the (A) interest threshold, whose consequence under
(C) is the amount wrongfully withheld plus fees. Rule 27 applies — the statute attaches consequences
to what the money is, so an education row alone leaves the landlord nothing on the face of the lease.
Written by the lead; `pet-policy` untouched. No new variables: `{{pet_deposit}}` and
`{{pet_rent_amount}}` already exist.

**11. `rent-concession` — the agent's cautious course accepted.** Where a concession makes rent
actually paid lower than stated rent, which figure is "one month's periodic rent" for the
R.C. 5321.16(A) interest threshold is unsettled on the text and no case law is held. Measuring
against the **stated, unconcessioned** rent is the higher and therefore safer figure, and the agent
stated it as such inside the tag note. Nothing further needed.

**12. Land installment contracts are in scope — no need to ask.** The B03 agent asked whether
Steinoak covers them, R.C. ch. 5313 being a real regime with hard cure periods that
R.C. 1923.02(A)(7) puts inside the eviction chapter. The corpus answers it: **ch. 5313 is one of the
eight core chapters for which the package ships the LSC's authenticated PDF of every section**
(5321, 1923, 4112, 1345, 5313, 5322, 5323, 4781), so the corpus builder treated it as in scope. The
agent's `edu-forfeiture-redemption-oh` stands.

**13. `edu-no-lockout-for-rent-oh` kept separate from `edu-self-help-eviction-ban-oh`.** Different
subjects under rule 57, and Ohio's answer to the opt-in question turns on R.C. 5321.13(A), which the
existing row does not cite. The pass's fence also bars changing an existing row, so merging is not
available here even if it were preferable.

**14. `notice-to-quit-waiver` kept at NEEDS_REVIEW, and this is the most interesting legal point of
the pass so far.** The agent asked whether a lease may waive or shorten the R.C. 1923.04(A) three-day
notice, and found that on the statutes alone the answer looks like **yes** — because R.C. 5321.13(A)
says "no provision of **this chapter**", which reaches R.C. 5321.17 and 5321.11 but **not**
R.C. 1923.04(A), and **chapter 1923 contains no non-waiver clause of its own**. The existing row
`edu-waiver-by-acceptance-oh` asserts the notice is jurisdictional, but its five district cases are
all about **acceptance of rent after** the notice, not a pre-dispute contractual waiver. The agent
wrote the cautious course into the row and set NEEDS_REVIEW rather than claim a prohibition the text
does not contain. That is exactly right, and I am not hardening it: rule 1 keeps cases as leads only,
so neither the row's conclusion nor its opposite can rest on them. Recorded here because the
structural finding — **the two notice regimes have different waiver rules** — is worth carrying into
every state's eviction work, not just Ohio's.

**15. `tenant-forward-proceedings` — tag only; no companion education row.** The agent offered to
draft one. Declined: Ohio's answer is that it has no forwarding statute, so an education row would
say only that, and the tag's OH note already says it at the point of use. The note correctly inverts
California's rationale — in Ohio the clause **creates** the duty rather than restating Cal. Civ. Code
§ 1949, and breach carries no statutory remedy, because R.C. 5321.05(C)(1)'s damages-plus-fees attach
only to a violation of R.C. 5321.05 itself and R.C. 5321.13(C) voids any fee agreement. That is a
rule-26 analysis done properly on a clause that merely *fits*.

**16. All four tags in these batches accepted after a rule-26 check**, each note naming the sibling it
rejected and why: `fees-and-charges-vi` (the VI deceptive-practices rationale does not transfer,
because R.C. 1345.01(A) reaches only "goods, a service, a franchise, or an intangible" and not a
lease of real property, but the closed list matters *more* in Ohio for exactly that reason);
`rent-concession-wi` (preferred over `rent-concession-az`, whose clawback would be exposed under
R.C. 5321.14, and over `rent-concessions-il`, which rests on the Illinois statute);
`tenant-caused-fines-as`; `tenant-forward-proceedings-ca`. One correction I made to the lead's own
delta: the `governmental-fines` note cited a **Colorado** provision as the thing Ohio lacks while
tagging the **American Samoa** row; it now names A.S.C.A. § 13.0224(c)(6), the row's actual basis,
alongside the Colorado provision. The substance was right; the citation was to the wrong jurisdiction.

**17. A process fix, after making the same mistake twice.** My batch briefs twice told an agent a
chapter was in the corpus when it was not — ch. 1319 to B04, and chs. 2505 and 2727 to B03. Both
agents caught it, returned `NOT_LOCATED` correctly, and reported the discrepancy, which is the system
working. But the briefs should not have been wrong. I have generated
`HELD_CHAPTERS.txt` from the section index — the 37 held chapters with their section counts — and
added an instruction to the standing briefing that the authoritative list is that file, that
`ohsearch.py ch <N>` returning "0 sections" means not held, and that **where a brief disagrees with
the corpus, the corpus wins**: flag it and carry on. Carried to Proposed SOP changes.

### Decisions on points B05-utilities and B06-disclosures raised

**18. Six `NOT_LOCATED` verdicts closed by the lead, every one of them.** Both agents handled the
unreachable material correctly — naming the chapter and the specific sections instead of guessing —
and in several cases guessed the section number right before I fetched it. What I fetched and what it
changed:

- **`utility-disconnection-notice-authorization` → PRESENT.** Ohio Adm.Code **4901:1-18-06**
  (eff. 2026-10-01) read whole. Two routes exist and one needs no tenant cooperation at all:
  (A)(3)(d) obliges the company, 'Upon the request of a property owner or the agent of a property
  owner', to give **three days' advance notice** before disconnecting, and (A)(3)(a) obliges every
  company to let a customer designate a third party, who gets **fourteen days**. (A)(3)(b) protects
  the designee from liability unless they separately agreed in writing to be a guarantor. This also
  answers the agent's companion-clause question in the affirmative — see item 19.
- **`municipal-utility-lien` → PRESENT, and it reverses the common assumption.** R.C. **743.04** read
  whole. A city may certify unpaid water rents to the tax list as a lien **only** 'if the auditor also
  receives … additional certification that the unpaid rents or charges have arisen pursuant to a
  service contract made directly with **an owner who occupies the property served**.' On a rental you
  do not occupy, the lien route is closed and the city is left with (A)(1)(b), an action at law
  against whoever is liable. (A)(3) gives an owner of record a removal remedy with four required
  contents. The agent's caution that the proviso may not repeat in the sewer chapters (729, 6117,
  6119) is right, is stated in the body, and is not resolved.
- **`lead-safe-certification` → PRESENT, at NEEDS_REVIEW.** R.C. **3742.41** and **3742.42** read
  whole. Ohio has a **free** lead-safe rental registry, voluntary for most owners and mandatory only
  for a unit under a lead hazard control order. But the registry runs 'in accordance with rules
  adopted under section 3742.45' and the voluntary route starts six months after those rules take
  effect — and Ohio Adm.Code **3701-32**, the lead chapter, holds only licensing, scope-of-practice,
  clearance, encapsulant, record-keeping, variance and hazard-level rules: **no registry rule, no
  lead-safe maintenance practice rule, no dust-sampling rule.** I did not search every OAC chapter or
  consult the Department of Health, so the row does not assert the rules were never adopted; it tells
  the landlord to confirm before relying on registration. That is the cautious course, in the body.
- **`lead-state-notices` → CONFIRMED_ABSENT**, bounded to the eight sections of ch. 3742 actually
  read rather than the chapter. Ohio's lead legislation works on the property and the contractor; the
  disclosure duty is federal and the shared `lead-based-paint` clause carries it.
- **`association-obligations-disclosure` → CONFIRMED_ABSENT.** R.C. **5311.26** read whole, which
  answers the exact question the agent framed: it is a **developer-to-purchaser** disclosure
  ('provides the prospective purchaser a condominium development disclosure statement') and reaches
  no tenant. The useful content is that R.C. 5311.19(A) binds the tenant directly anyway.
- **`hazardous-contamination-disclosure` → CONFIRMED_ABSENT.** R.C. **5301.85** and **5301.88** read
  whole. Ohio's Uniform Environmental Covenants Act works by recording, not by notice — but
  R.C. 5301.85(B)(5) makes a covenant enforceable even though it 'imposes an affirmative obligation
  on a person **having an interest in the real property**', which a tenant has. So a tenant can be
  bound by a limitation nobody ever shows them.
- **`shoreline-access-disclosure` → CONFIRMED_ABSENT.** R.C. **1506.07** read whole: a
  construction-permit regime for Lake Erie coastal erosion areas whose only notification limb runs to
  local government, with (D) letting an approved county or municipal ordinance displace the state
  permit.
- **`pool-safety`** — R.C. **3749.01** read whole and handed back to the B08 agent, which still had
  the batch open. The line is a family count: (F) a pool at 'a dwelling housing **no more than three
  families**' used only by residents and nonpaying guests is private and outside R.C. 3749.01 to
  3749.10; (G) a pool 'intended to be used collectively' and operated by an owner or lessee
  'regardless of whether or not a fee is charged' is a **public swimming pool**, licensed by the
  board of health. So four or more families means a licence.

**19. Two more companion clauses: one added, one declined.** The B05 agent offered both.
**Added `utility-shutoff-notice-designee-oh`**, because rule 27 is squarely on point — Ohio Adm.Code
4901:1-18-06(A)(3)(a) obliges the *company* to permit a designation but obliges nobody to make one,
so the designation is the tenant's act and without a lease term the landlord cannot get it. Two
deliberate departures from `utility-shutoff-notice-designee-vi`: it names no utility (a municipal
water department is outside these rules entirely under R.C. 4905.02(A)(3)), and it carries an
anti-guarantor sentence Ohio needs and the sibling does not, because (A)(3)(b) makes the distinction
between designee and guarantor the thing that decides liability. **Declined `energy-data-release-oh`**:
the agent's own education row establishes Ohio gives no whole-building energy-data right, the
authorization it would require is the serving utility's own form rather than anything the lease
creates, and R.C. 4928 — the likeliest home of such a right — was not read. Adding a clause to
procure a form whose existence I have not verified would be drafting ahead of the research.

**20. The R.C. 1345.01(A) fork — chased, and now bounded rather than open.** The agent flagged, in
four rows, that R.C. 1345.01(A) excludes 'transactions between persons, defined in sections 4905.03
and 5725.01 of the Revised Code, and their customers', so a landlord marking up a resold utility is
either a public utility or a CSPA supplier, with neither comfortable. It asked whether to chase it. I
read **R.C. 4905.02** and **R.C. 4905.03** whole, and the position is this. Each 4905.03 limb requires
being 'engaged in the business of supplying' the commodity 'to consumers within this state', which a
landlord whose business is letting arguably is not — but the text does not settle it and no case law
or PUCO decision is in the corpus. Two things are now settled, though. **R.C. 4905.02(A)(3) excludes a
utility 'owned or operated by any municipal corporation' from 'public utility'**, so a city water
department is outside PUCO's jurisdiction — but because R.C. 1345.01(A) keys on the **4905.03
definitions** rather than on 4905.02's label, that same department is still a 'water-works company'
under 4905.03(G) and the CSPA exclusion still reaches it. And the **vetoed H.B. 173 proposed to add a
submetered-provider exclusion at R.C. 4905.02(A)(6)** — the General Assembly thought an exclusion was
needed, which presupposes submetered providers may currently fall inside. That is the best evidence
available that the question is live. The agent's handling — a flagged caution in every affected row,
never an answer — stands; it is now a caution with a read analysis behind it.

**21. `edu-utility-deposit-is-security-deposit-oh` kept flat, not hedged.** The agent asked whether to
soften it to 'should be treated as'. No. R.C. 5321.01(E)'s 'any deposit of money **or property** to
secure performance' is deliberately broad and enumerates no types; a utility deposit the landlord
holds plainly secures performance, so R.C. 5321.16 governs it. Hedging would suggest a live argument
the text does not offer. One refinement the row should carry and I have flagged rather than rewritten:
a deposit the **utility company** holds is not the landlord's deposit at all, and R.C. 5321.16 cannot
reach it.

**22. `{{utility_allowance_amount}}` is genuinely new — the pass's first and only new variable.** The
agent could not see the catalogue and asked me to reconcile. I checked: the library already has
`utility_admin_fee`, `utility_admin_fees`, `utility_avg_bill`, `utility_bill_copy_fee`,
`utility_billing_method`, `utility_deposit`, `utility_high_bill`, `utility_low_bill`,
`utility_provider_name` and `utility_provider_phone`, and no allowance variable of any spelling.
`{{utility_allowance_amount}}` does not collide and is kept.

**23. The three `CONFIRMED_ABSENT` labels the B06 agent offered to relabel are left as they are.**
It flagged `asbestos-hazards`, `private-well-testing` and `sprinkler-disclosure` as rows where a
strict reading of rule 36 might prefer `NOT_LOCATED`, because a collateral chapter is unheld. In each
the unread material governs contractors, installers or new construction rather than a disclosure to a
tenant, the tenancy-side question is answerable inside the fully held ch. 5321, and the boundary is
named in the body as well as the notes. Rule 36 separates 'the subject lies outside the corpus' from
'the subject is inside and Ohio has no provision'; here the subject is inside. Each row names its gap
in `chapters_needed` regardless, so nothing is lost if Claude Code disagrees at sync.

**24. A second possible defect in `hoa-compliance`, found by the B06 agent and worth separating from
the first.** R.C. 5311.19(B)(1) lets the association sue only 'as the unit owner's agent, in the name
of the unit owner'. If that clause — or any Ohio clause — lets an association act **in its own name**
against a tenant, that is a distinct problem from the R.C. 5321.13(D) indemnity defect already
reported, and it would need its own correction. I have not re-read the shared clause to check, because
the pass's fence bars re-auditing existing rows; recorded here so Claude Code can look at both at
sync.

**25. A mistake of my own, caught by a tool I built because of it.** Writing the
`association-obligations-disclosure` row I recorded battery hit counts **from memory instead of
running them** — 'condominium' 19, 'declaration' 11, 'bylaws' 9, 'unit owners association' 0. The real
counts are 5, 16, 2 and 1. I noticed only because I ran the terms afterwards to check. That is
precisely the failure rule 19's battery discipline exists to prevent: an unrun battery is not evidence
that the instrument was answering, and a row carrying one is unfalsifiable. So I wrote
`verify_batteries.py`, which re-runs **every** term in **every** saved battery against the corpus and
reports each count that does not reproduce. Over the 117 answers then saved it re-ran 353 terms and
surfaced 35 discrepancies. Thirty were legitimate scope — a term searched over the OAC, the Fire Code
rule PDFs or enrolled act text does not reproduce against the 37 held chapters, and the agents had
said so in the entry. **Six were real and are now corrected**: `nonrefundable` recorded 0 in both
`pet-fees` and `required-fees` when it returns 3; `rebate` 0 in `rent-concession` when it returns 6;
`inducement` 0 there when it returns 1; `inundation` 0 in `flood-disclosure` when it returns 2; and
`security instrument` 2 in `rent-receipt-anti-waiver` when it returns 3. I read every one of those
sections: R.C. 4501.34, 4781.10 and 4781.281 for 'nonrefundable' (BMV and manufactured-home-commission
fees), R.C. 169.01, 1309.616, 1345.02, 1349.27, 5162.50 and 5162.52 for 'rebate', R.C. 1345.02 for
'inducement', R.C. 3729.01 and 4781.01 for 'inundation'. **None changes any conclusion** — but each
row now records what was actually run and what the hits actually are, and says the lead corrected it.
The verifier is now part of the independent check, and a proposed SOP change.

### Decisions on points B08-safety raised

**26. All six questions settled; one needed research and got it.**

- **`{{alarm_tampering_fee}}` left blank.** The agent's own recommendation, and right: Ohio sets no
  figure and no cap, R.C. 5321.14 unconscionability is the only ceiling, and seeding a number would
  give the library a figure to defend with no Ohio source behind it.
- **No `common-area-lighting` cost-allocation clause.** R.C. 5321.04(A)(3) and (A)(4) put the duty on
  the landlord and R.C. 5321.13(A) and (F) keep it there — duty-shifting runs one way only, and a
  clause allocating the *cost* of a duty the landlord cannot shift invites the R.C. 5321.13 problem
  rather than solving anything. Education row only.
- **No separate `security-devices` clause.** The agent could not read the shared `keys` clause's body;
  I read it for Ohio item 3 and can answer definitively. `keys` already covers re-keying at turnover
  and charging its cost, which I confirmed is lawful in Ohio (nothing in R.C. 5321.04's ten limbs
  gives a tenant any right to keys or locks, so there is no chapter right to waive, and
  R.C. 5321.16(B) expressly allows applying the deposit to it). Tenant-added locks are covered by
  `no-alterations`, which Ohio item 4 confirms reads correctly for Ohio. Both halves are already
  there; a third clause would duplicate them.
- **No `rental-inspection` clause.** It would be premised on a local inspection regime that rule 3
  keeps flagged and never resolved — drafting on an unread basis. The shared access-and-entry clause
  already obliges the tenant to permit lawful entry, and the education row carries the local point.
- **`pool-safety`'s three-family line is a finding, not an edit.** The agent is right that
  R.C. 3749.01(F)'s 'no more than three families' is a sixth Ohio small-landlord threshold alongside
  the three-or-fewer-units escrow exemption and R.C. 3781.06(C)(9)'s definition of a residential
  building. `edu-portfolio-thresholds-oh` is an existing row and the pass's fence bars changing it, so
  this goes to Claude Code at sync under findings rather than into the row.
- **`smart-access` — the one that needed research, and I did it rather than deferring it.** The agent
  asked whether biometric and entry-log credentials engage R.C. ch. 1345 or ch. 1349, both held, or
  whether that belongs to a later batch. It belongs here, and the answer is a clean negative worth
  having: **'biometric' returns zero sections across all 37 held chapters**, so Ohio has no biometric
  privacy statute of the Illinois kind, and **R.C. 1349.19(A)(7)(a)** — read section-open — defines
  'personal information' as a name combined with a social security number, a driver's licence or state
  identification card number, or an account or card number with its security code. **A fingerprint,
  face or iris template is not in that list**, so a landlord holding smart-access biometrics has no
  R.C. 1349.19 notification duty for that data as such — though the duty does attach to the three
  listed elements, which an ordinary tenant file routinely contains. R.C. 1345.01(A) keeps the CSPA
  away from a lease in any event. Added to the row's notes with its supporting battery, every hit
  checked including the single 'retina' hit (R.C. 2929.01, criminal sentencing definitions).

**27. Two findings from this batch that change how other rows should read.** Neither is an error in an
existing row and neither was edited.

- **R.C. 3781.103** — 'Deadbolt locks or equivalent security locking device for swinging exit doors',
  eff. 1975-09-05, S.B. 101, 111th G.A. — is Ohio's only security-device statute and no brief of mine
  named it. The agent found it and read it section-open. It directs the board of building standards
  to require a deadbolt or equivalent on each unit's swinging exit door in a multi-unit dwelling, **but
  only for buildings 'for which plans are approved pursuant to section 3791.04'** — plan approval, not
  retrofit. Chapter 3791 is not held.
- **R.C. 3781.06(C)(9)–(10) splits the whole safety batch by building size.** A 'residential building'
  is a one-, two- or three-family dwelling house, so a four-or-more-unit apartment building is a
  *nonresidential* building governed by Ohio Adm.Code 4101:1 (Building Code) while one-to-three-family
  dwellings fall under 4101:8 (Residential Code). **Neither was readable**, so every affected row says
  in its body which code governs it and that the code could not be read.

**28. Ohio item 6 is now as closed as this corpus allows, and the answer is mostly a negative.**
'Carbon monoxide' returns **zero sections** across all 37 held chapters — a hard negative. R.C. 3781.104
is high-rise-only and has no CO provision. The Fire Code rules in the corpus do carry alarm and CO
content, but they print only Ohio's modifications to the International Fire Code, and two of those
modifications matter more than the rest: **Ohio Adm.Code 1301:7-7-01 item 6**, amending OFC
102.1.1(4), provides that 'If a distinct hazard to life or property cannot be proven by the fire
official by a preponderance of the evidence, the provisions of this code shall not apply to an
existing building or condition' — the sharpest limit on the Fire Code's reach over a sitting landlord,
and Ohio's own text rather than the ICC's; and **OFC 1103.9 as Ohio amends it** inserts 'On or before
January 1, 2019,' before 'carbon', so Ohio did set a CO retrofit deadline, though **which occupancies
it reaches is ICC base text and unreadable**, so the row claims only the date and the fact of
applicability. The remaining gap is a single document: **Ohio Adm.Code 4101:8-3-01**, PDF-only and
text-free, holding IRC R314 smoke alarms, R315 CO alarms, R312 guards and window fall protection and
R303 light. An OCR pass over that one PDF would close the item outright, and that is the
recommendation to carry forward.

### Decisions on points B07-dv-protected raised

**29. Ohio item 2 is closed, and the answer is a confirmed absence with an unusually clean battery.**
`dv-lockchange` is `CONFIRMED_ABSENT`. Chapter 5321's only two uses of "domestic violence" are the
shelter-exclusion limbs at R.C. 5321.01(C)(9)(b)(ii) and (C)(10); chapters 5321 and 1923 contain no
lock, rekey or DV provision; `rekey`, `change the locks` and `lock change` all return **0**, against
`victim` 79, `abuse` 75 and `survivor` 12 as positives with the control at 0. R.C. 3113.31 contains
the words "lock", "landlord", "lessor" and "rental agreement" **nowhere** — a negative I read myself
and the agent relied on as reported, saying so rather than claiming to have verified an
out-of-corpus whole-section negative, which is the right discipline. Ohio's only dwelling-lock
statute is R.C. 3781.103, a board-of-building-standards deadbolt rule for **new** multi-unit plans
approved under R.C. 3791.04, not a sitting-landlord duty. What the row does carry is the real
mechanism: R.C. 3113.31(E)(1)(b) and (c) let the court reallocate **possession** three ways depending
on who holds the lease, which a landlord must honour even though it is not a landlord duty, and
R.C. 5321.15(A) bars the landlord acting on a victim's request alone. **`edu-no-dv-lease-termination-oh`
still holds**, all three limbs independently confirmed, and was not changed.

**30. The renumbering question was answered with a better instrument than the one I suggested.**
I briefed the agent to check whether R.C. 3113.31 had been renumbered, since the pass had already
found two instances of a 2026-03-20 wave. It checked the **enrolled acts** rather than the chapter
page: "Sec. 3113.31" appears in **zero of the 215 enrolled acts** of the 135th G.A., its 2024 special
session and the 136th — and because an Ohio act prints every section it amends under a `Sec. N.`
heading, that is affirmative evidence nothing touched the text or the number. Thirteen of those acts
cross-reference it *by that number*, and the drafters do flag former numbers elsewhere ("former
section 3113.21"), with no act saying "former section 3113.31". **Not moved.** The same technique
corroborated the 955 renumbering from inside the corpus: R.C. 2921.321(E)(4), eff. 2026-03-20,
H.B. 247, now points to "section 955.021".

**31. Two rows held at NEEDS_REVIEW on one identified fact each. I chased both; both are now
VERIFIED, and both answers changed the row.**

- **`dv-confidentiality`.** The agent said a disclosure offence reaching "any person" would change
  its "no landlord duty" conclusion. I fetched **R.C. 111.99** and there is one: **(D)** binds "No
  person who obtains a confidential address or telephone number from the secretary of state under
  division (E) of section 111.43", and **(F)** makes a violation a **misdemeanor of the first
  degree**. (B)(2) is confined to a "public official" as (B)(1) defines it and (C) to the law
  enforcement gateway, neither of which reaches a landlord. So the accurate statement is not "no
  landlord duty" but **"no duty unless you take the authorized route, and then a criminal one"** — if
  the tenant authorises disclosure to the landlord under R.C. 111.43(E)(1)(c), the landlord becomes a
  person who obtained it under division (E) and knowing re-disclosure is an offence. That is exactly
  why R.C. 111.43(E)(3) requires the warning "You are not permitted to redisclose the following
  information for any reason." Also a **range correction to my own brief**: the programme is
  R.C. 111.41 **to 111.99**, not 111.47 — R.C. 1901.25(A)(2) says so and R.C. 2929.18(B)(11) puts its
  fund at 111.48.
- **`tenant-screening`.** The agent read R.C. 2953.26 from enrolled act text the corpus holds,
  confirmed no later act prints "Sec. 2953.26", but could not obtain **H.B. 50's effective date** —
  and was right to refuse to assume, noting that the vetoed H.B. 173 also appears on the acts list, so
  listing is not proof of enactment. I fetched the section's own page: **Effective September 20,
  2024, enacted by House Bill 50, 135th G.A.**, sole Available Version. It is law. And the content is
  the opposite of what landlords assume: a **certificate of qualification for housing** does not force
  anyone to rent. It lifts the automatic bar, the landlord "shall consider on a case-by-case basis"
  (D)(1), the certificate is only a rebuttable presumption and the landlord "may deny the housing ...
  if it determines that the person is unfit" (D)(2) — and in exchange, **(F)(2) gives the landlord
  immunity in a negligent-leasing tort action where the landlord knew of the certificate**, with
  (F)(1) making it evidence of due care. "Decision-maker" expressly includes "a landlord as defined
  in section 1923.01" (A)(2). The trap is (F)(3): retaining a tenant after demonstrated dangerousness
  or a later felony or violent misdemeanour exposes the landlord, but only on proof of **both** actual
  knowledge and willfulness.

**32. Both clauses the agent proposed are built, and both were right to propose.**

- **`disability-modification-oh`** under `disability-accommodation`. Rule 27 squarely: the three
  conditions in R.C. 4112.02(H)(18)(a) exist only if the landlord imposes them, and Ohio Adm.Code
  4112-5-07(D) makes the restoration duty turn on the agreement having been made — "if it was
  reasonable for the landlord to have obtained an agreement ... **and such an agreement was
  obtained**, the interior modifications shall be removed at the person with a disability's expense".
  Without the agreement there is no restoration duty at all, so education alone leaves the landlord
  nothing. The clause carries all four statutory limits including the (b) prohibition on requiring a
  larger-than-customary deposit, and states **no figure** — the escrow is measured by the projected
  restoration cost, which is the statute's own measure. **One interaction flagged, not resolved,
  with the cautious course in the clause:** whether that escrow is also a "security deposit" under
  R.C. 5321.01(E) is unsettled, and the two regimes differ — R.C. 5321.16(A) pays five per cent on an
  excess after six months while R.C. 4112.02(H)(18)(a)(iii) gives the tenant **all** the interest from
  the start. The specific should govern the general, so the clause uses the 4112 terms and requires
  the account to be held and accounted for separately from the security deposit, which is safe either
  way.
- **`drug-activity-prohibition-oh`** under `drug-free-housing-addendum`. The agent's case is the
  right one: Ohio conditions nothing on the lease for the statutory route, **but R.C. 1923.02(A)(9)**
  — which I read section-open — gives a ground "Against tenants who have breached an obligation
  imposed upon them by a **written rental agreement**", so writing the prohibition down creates an
  independent ground that does not depend on the R.C. 1923.02(A)(6)(a)(i) proof structure. Both
  constraints the agent named are observed: the clause does **not** say "Landlord may terminate",
  because R.C. 5321.17(C) says the landlord "shall terminate" and R.C. 5321.04(A)(9) then requires
  prompt commencement — so the body tells the tenant Ohio law **requires** the landlord to act and
  that there is no discretion to overlook it; and it does **not** shift the duty, R.C. 5321.13(F)
  barring a landlord from assuming the R.C. 5321.05(A)(9) obligation, which the body says expressly.
  The only period named is the statute's three days.

**33. Questions 3, 4 and 5 are not mine to answer and go where they belong.** R.C. 2953.26 deserving
its own topic, and whether the Fair Credit Reporting Act should be one shared `FEDERAL:` row or a
sentence per state, are both **Proposed topic questions** — they change the library's shape, not
Ohio's law. How to serve a tenant whose only address of record is the Secretary of State's would
require changing an existing eviction row, which the pass's fence bars; it goes to **§6** for Claude
Code, with the material: R.C. 111.43(D)(4)(a) makes service on the Secretary service under Civ.R. 4.2,
but a R.C. 1923.04(A) notice to leave is **not** service of process, so the two do not obviously
connect and the point needs its own look.

**34. One existing row's standing caveat is discharged, and one sentence of it overstates.** The agent
read R.C. 4112.02 whole plus 4112.08, 4112.16 and 4112.99 and searched "exempt" and "shall not
apply", which discharges `edu-portfolio-thresholds-oh`'s own note that the rest of chapter 4112 had
not been read. The row is right that no size test exempts anyone from the core housing prohibitions.
But its sentence "Fair housing under Chapter 4112 has NO small landlord exemption at all" is written
at chapter level, and the chapter has **two** size tests: **R.C. 4112.01(A)(2)** defines "employer" as
four or more persons, so a landlord with three employees is outside division (A) altogether; and
**R.C. 4112.02(H)(22)** imposes the design-and-construct duty only on "covered multifamily dwellings",
meaning buildings of four or more units with first occupancy on or after 30 June 1992. Both count
four, like three of the row's existing five thresholds. Reported, not edited.

**35. Two internal tensions recorded rather than resolved, both worth a sync look.**
**R.C. 4112.02(H)(17)** permits five applicant inquiries, two of which **Ohio Adm.Code 4112-5-07(B)**
does not except — the statute prevails over the rule, but the rule is the safe course, and both rows
say so. And **Ohio Adm.Code 4112-5-04(B)** cites "division (H)(7)" for the record-keeping safe
harbour where the prohibition now sits at **(H)(8)** — a stale cross-reference in the rule, flagged
and not resolved.

### Decisions on points B09-termination raised

**36. `lease-term-limitation` resolved, verdict changed, and the answer is a threshold nobody drafting
a long Ohio lease would expect.** The agent returned `NOT_LOCATED` having proved the no-cap half from
inside the corpus and correctly named R.C. 5301.01 as the missing formalities limb. I fetched it —
and also **R.C. 5301.08, which the agent had not named and which is the whole answer.**
R.C. 5301.01(A) requires a 'lease of any interest in real property' to be signed by the lessor and
**acknowledged** before a judge, clerk of a court of record, county auditor, county engineer, notary
public or mayor. Read alone that would require every apartment lease to be notarised. R.C. 5301.08
lifts the entire block — 'Sections 5301.01 to 5301.45 … do not affect the validity of any lease … of
any other lands **for any term not exceeding three years** or require that lease to be acknowledged
or recorded.' **So three years or less needs nothing; longer than three years should be notarised and
recorded.** Verdict **PRESENT**, and I renamed the row `edu-lease-term-and-formalities-oh` because the
subject turned out to be formalities rather than a cap.

That gives Ohio **three lease-term thresholds** worth carrying elsewhere in the library: **one year**
(R.C. 1335.05 bars the *action* on an agreement not to be performed within a year absent a signed
writing — note it bars the action, not the agreement), **three years** (acknowledgment and recording),
and **no ceiling at all** on the term itself. It is a *term* threshold rather than a portfolio one, so
it is logged as a finding rather than added to `edu-portfolio-thresholds-oh`, which the fence bars
changing.

**37. `tenant-death-contact-oh` written, and the agent was right not to tag.** It declined to tag
`tenant-death-contact-nh` because that clause's closing sentence promises to handle property left
behind 'under this Lease's Handling of Property Left Behind Section' — and every state tagging it
answers that pointer with its own abandoned-property clause, which **Ohio does not have**. Tagging
would have planted a dangling cross-reference in an Ohio lease. That is rule 26's principle reaching
further than its letter: a shared clause can fit in wording and still be wrong for a state whose
surrounding structure differs. Lead decision: write the Ohio clause with the pointer replaced by
Ohio's real route — deal with the personal representative on the chapter 2117 timetable, and act only
as a court orders.

**38. No Ohio abandoned-property clause, and the reason is legal rather than cautious.** The agent's
third question. Ohio gives **no safe harbour**: R.C. 1923.13(A)'s ordinary writ contains no notice,
valuation or disposal machinery at all — every bit of that sits in (B) with R.C. 1923.12 and 1923.14,
which run only against a manufactured-home park resident or the resident's estate — and 'abandon'
appears **nowhere** in chapter 5321. A lease clause purporting to give the landlord a disposal
procedure would therefore be inventing authority the statute withholds, and the exposure it creates
is a conversion claim that **R.C. 5321.13(D) forbids the lease to indemnify the landlord against**.
So a disposal clause would simultaneously overstate the landlord's power and be unable to protect
them for using it. The right output is the education row plus the instruction to get a court order,
which is what Ohio now has. Recorded with the reasoning so a later pass does not re-litigate it on
instinct.

**39. `holdover-oh` is structurally unlike every other state's, and that is for Claude Code, not for
me.** The agent found that Ohio prices its holdover **inside** the base clause using
