`{{holdover_daily_rate}}`, where the Virgin Islands and American Samoa keep a bare `Holdover` clause
plus a separate optional rate clause. It asked whether Ohio should be restructured to the split. I am
not restructuring an existing row — the pass's fence bars it, and the agent's education row works
either way, which was the right thing to build. Logged under findings so the shape question can be
decided once across the library rather than state by state.

**40. One existing row's flag can be cleared.** `edu-retaliation-oh`'s notes say R.C. 5321.03 was 'NOT
read section-open in this pass, flagged'. The agent read it section-open (eff. 2023-10-03, H.B. 33,
135th G.A.) and it bears the row out, including the (B)(3) termination remedy the row already states.
The flag can come off at sync.

**41. `edu-no-abandoned-property-safe-harbor-oh` reconfirmed a second time, from a different
direction.** The eviction batch confirmed it park-only from the chapter structure; the termination
batch confirmed it again from **the writ text itself**, which is the stronger reading. Two independent
confirmations of the same row by two agents that did not see each other's work.

**42. The battery practice has propagated, which is the most encouraging thing in this batch.** The
B09 agent wrote **its own** verification script to re-run every battery term against `ohsearch.py`
before saving, and caught and fixed **two of its own wrong counts** in `landlord-remedies-termination`
without being asked. Its batch came back with **zero** mismatches. That is the fix for my own error
in §4 item 25 working one level down, and it is worth making standing: the proposed SOP change should
require the re-run of the agent as well as of the lead.

### Decisions on points B10-prohibited raised

**43. Ohio item 1 is closed, both halves PRESENT.** `smoke-drift-waiver` turns on R.C. 5321.13(D),
which bars any tenant agreement to the exculpation or limitation of "any liability of the landlord
arising under law" **with no common-areas exception** — so a clause making the tenant accept drifting
smoke or waive a claim about it is unrecognised to the extent the landlord's R.C. 5321.04(A)(1)–(4)
duties are engaged. `eviction-penalty-clause-ban` splits three ways, which is why it needed its own
row: R.C. 5321.13(C) kills anything that is really attorney's fees **however relabelled**;
**R.C. 1923.09(A) already gives the costs of suit by statute**, so a clause is unnecessary for that;
and a genuinely **non-fee** eviction penalty is prohibited nowhere in the corpus and is capped only by
R.C. 5321.14.

**44. The brief I wrote predicted a split that does not exist, and the agent corrected me.** I told it
to ask whether Ohio bars waiving the homestead exemption in advance, expecting the usual pattern
(collection exemption waivable, household basics not). **R.C. 2329.661(B) is categorical**: "every
promise, agreement, or contract insofar as it seeks to waive the exemption laws of this state is
void." Neither is waivable. A bonus the agent found: **R.C. 2329.66(A)(3) expressly names a landlord
as a depositary**, so a tenant's deposit in the landlord's hands is exempt money up to $400 — which
bears on any attempt to apply a deposit against a judgment.

**45. R.C. 5321.131(D) is the only place Ohio deems a specific lease clause unconscionable per se**,
and `edu-flag-display-oh` states the flag prohibition without that consequence. The agent carried it
in `edu-unconscionability-oh` rather than editing the flag row, which is right under the fence, and
recorded it as a non-error note.

**46. R.C. 1345.031 does not reach a lease, twice over** — (A) confines the section to residential
**mortgage origination** and (C)(1) to a "mortgage loan contract", on top of R.C. 1345.01(A)'s
goods/service/franchise/intangible subject matter. Same for **Ohio Adm.Code 109:4-3-28**, whose
paragraph (C)(7) carries Ohio's only statutory liquidated-damages test and is mortgage-only. That
closes the consumer-protection route for this whole family, and it is worth having proved rather than
assumed, because `liquidated-damages` and `unconscionability` would both otherwise look like they had
a statutory test to apply.

**47. The sharpest conflict in the pass, and I answered the agent's question with a no.**
`purpose-limitation` sits on a genuine statute-against-statute collision: **R.C. 3767.10** (eff.
1953-10-01) makes a lease void at the owner's option where the tenant uses the place for lewdness,
assignation or prostitution, and says the owner "may without process of law make immediate entry upon
the premises" — while **R.C. 5321.15(A)** (eff. 1974-11-04) bars a landlord of residential premises
from any act of exclusion to recover possession "other than as provided in Chapters 1923., 5303., and
5321." The agent wrote the cautious course, held the row at NEEDS_REVIEW, and asked whether to
escalate for a case-law check.

**No — and the reason is rule 1.** Case law is a lead only, never load-bearing, so a case could
neither verify this row nor refute it; a row is verified on the text or not at all. I read both
sections section-open, and the text resolves it on three independent grounds. **The enumeration**,
which the agent itself spotted and which is textual rather than a canon: R.C. 5321.15(A) lists the
permitted routes and chapter 3767 is not among them, so the statute that enumerates excludes this one
by its own terms. **Later and more specific**: 1974 against 1953, "residential premises" and
recovering possession against "a building or tenement" generally. **Non-waivable**: R.C. 5321.13(A)
bars modifying any provision of chapter 5321 except through (F), so a tenant cannot consent to the
3767.10 route. And R.C. 5321.15(C) prices the error at all damages plus reasonable attorney's fees.
What survives of R.C. 3767.10 for a residential tenancy is its **voiding** limb — avoiding the lease
is not an act against the tenant to recover possession — and what does not survive is the self-help
entry. **The row stays NEEDS_REVIEW, but for a different reason than it was given**: not that the law
is unsettled, which I am satisfied it is not, but because this is the pass's only genuine collision
between two Ohio statutes on the same physical act, one of them purporting in terms to authorise
entry without process of law, and a human should lay eyes on it once.

**48. No jury-waiver clause.** The agent asked. R.C. 1923.09(A) makes an Ohio eviction a bench trial
unless a party demands a jury **on the return day**, so a waiver clause buys very little; whether a
pre-dispute waiver of the Ohio Const. art. I, § 5 right is enforceable depends on case law that rule 1
keeps as a lead; and R.C. 1901.24(B) routes waiver through the Civil Rules, which are not in the
corpus. A clause would trade a real drafting risk for a benefit the default already supplies.
Education only.

**49. No arbitration clause in this pass, and this one I would revisit.** The agent asked for a
money-only Ohio arbitration clause with an express possession carve-out. The statutory support is
genuinely there — **R.C. 2711.01(B)(1)(b)** puts "controversies involving the amount of rentals due
under any lease" expressly inside the Act while (B)(1) excludes possession — so this is not a
no-in-principle. It is a no-for-now on two unresolved points the agent itself named: whether
(B)(1)'s "**do not apply**" leaves a possession-arbitration agreement enforceable by some other route,
which needs R.C. 2711.02 and 2711.03; and whether **FEDERAL: the Federal Arbitration Act, 9 U.S.C. 1
et seq.**, preempts the state carve-out for a lease affecting interstate commerce. An arbitration
clause is also a consequential choice for a landlord — it can cost the municipal court's fast
forcible-entry track — so drafting one ahead of those answers would be exactly the error I avoided on
`energy-data-release-oh`. Carried as a candidate for the moment ch. 2711's remaining sections and FAA
preemption are settled.

**50. `optional-lease-terms` produced the hard proof of a claim the framing file had only asserted.**
`OHIO_FRAMING.md` says Ohio has "almost no 'if the rental agreement so provides' hooks". The agent
established it: **"unless otherwise agreed" appears in only two sections of the entire corpus**
(R.C. 1309.207 and 3721.081), neither landlord-tenant. Ohio's real lease hooks are a short closed
list — R.C. 5321.05(A)(7), 5321.07(C), 1923.02(A)(9), 5321.13(F), 3796.04(iii) and 1343.03(A) — and
having that list proved rather than asserted is worth more than the row it sits in, because every
"may the lease do X?" topic in the library turns on it.

### Decisions on points B11-occupancy and B12-access raised

**51. The access-batch agent corrected my brief on a point of law, and it was right.** My brief told
it that **R.C. 5321.04(B)** "gives the landlord an injunction and damages where a tenant unreasonably
withholds." That is wrong. Read whole, **(B) is the TENANT's remedy** — on an (A)(8) violation, a
lawful entry made "in an unreasonable manner", or "repeated demands for entry otherwise lawful that
have the effect of harassing the tenant", the **tenant** may recover actual damages, obtain
injunctive relief, recover reasonable attorney's fees, or terminate. The landlord's access remedy is
**R.C. 5321.05(C)(1)** — actual damages plus statutory fees, plus "injunctive relief to compel access
under division (B) of this section", which means **R.C. 5321.05(B)**, the tenant's duty not to
withhold consent unreasonably. I had conflated two different divisions (B). **I checked every row in
the delta that cites R.C. 5321.04(B): all fifteen describe it correctly as the tenant's remedy, so
the error never left the brief.** It is now recorded as a standing correction in `OHIO_FRAMING.md` so
no later batch inherits it.

**52. A worse one of mine did reach rows, and is now fixed in all of them.** `OHIO_FRAMING.md` said
R.C. 5321.02 retaliation is "bounded by R.C. 5321.03's **four** grounds". **It has five** — (A)(5) is
the registered-offender ground for premises within one thousand feet of school, preschool, child
care, children's crisis care or residential infant care premises, added by Am. Sub. H.B. 33, 135th
G.A. The B12 agent read the section section-open and caught it. Because the error was in a **shared
file every agent reads**, it propagated: four rows had copied the wrong count
(`edu-no-good-cause-notice-oh`, `edu-statutory-early-termination-oh`, `edu-tenant-organizing-oh`, and
the Ohio note on the `owner-occupancy-termination-ar` tag). The framing file and all four rows are
corrected, each row carrying a note saying what was wrong and who caught it. **This is the strongest
argument in the pass for the instruction that where a brief or a framing file disagrees with the
corpus, the corpus wins** — two agents have now corrected me on a shared document and both were
right.

**53. The agent reported two of my own rows as "does not exist", and it was looking in the right
place.** B12 flagged `tenant-death-contact-oh` and `drug-activity-prohibition-oh` as ids in my brief
that it could not find. Both exist — I wrote them as `extra_rows` under rule 57's
clause-plus-education pattern, and an agent reading a sibling answer's `row` field cannot see an
`extra_rows` entry. That is a real discoverability gap in my own pipeline, and the route by which a
duplicate row could have slipped in. Fixed: `mkpassrows.py` now generates
**`pass_rows_so_far.md`** — every row this pass has added, `extra_rows` included, with its type,
group, status, topic_key and batch, plus the shared rows tagged with OH — and the standing briefing
now tells every agent to read it alongside `oh_existing_rows.md` before drafting. 187 rows and 8 tags
at the time of writing.

**54. A row THIS PASS wrote was incomplete, and the fence does not protect it.**
`edu-no-environmental-event-termination-oh`, written in the termination batch, described the
R.C. 5321.07 habitability route without **(C)**'s three-or-fewer-units exemption or **(D)**'s
student-tenant exemption, so it read as though the escrow route is always open to the tenant. The B12
agent caught it reading an earlier batch's row — the canvass checking itself. Because this is the
pass's own row rather than an existing one, I fixed it rather than reporting it: both exemptions are
now in the body, together with the **condition inside (C)** (the exemption depends on the landlord
having given notice of the fact, in the written lease or at initial occupancy for an oral tenancy)
and with the point that (C) and (D) withdraw **that section only**, so R.C. 5321.12, 1923.061(B),
5321.04(B), 5321.15(C) and 1923.15 all survive. That last point is the same incompleteness the pass
has already reported in two *existing* rows, so this row must not repeat it.

**55. `portable-solar` resolved — the pass's last `NOT_LOCATED`, and the answer is a negative worth
having.** The B11 agent refused to name a section inside chapters 4928 or 5301 rather than cite from
recollection, which was exactly right — and I then made that very mistake, guessing R.C. 5301.254 and
getting the nonresident-alien filing provision. Searching the chapter index instead found
**R.C. 5301.63** at once: 'any person may grant a solar access easement', in writing, with five
specified contents, running with the land, enforceable by the benefited owner against obstruction.
**It is an enabling provision for a voluntary easement between landowners about sunlight. It creates
no tenant right and voids nothing** — unlike California's and Arizona's solar-rights statutes — and a
tenant has no interest to burden, so cannot grant one over the landlord's land. So Ohio has no solar
rights and the lease decides. `CONFIRMED_ABSENT`, with chapter 4928 named as a utility-side limb that
cannot create a tenant right against a lease ban.

**56. And I made the battery mistake a second time, in that same row.** I wrote 'renewable' as 0 hits
in both the body and the notes; the counter returns **4** (R.C. 2329.01, 3781.07, 4503.44, 4503.182 —
levy and sale, board employees, parking placards, temporary registration, none of them relevant). I
caught it only because I had the script print the battery as it ran. The conclusion is unchanged and
the count is now honest, with the correction recorded in the row. Twice in one pass is a pattern, not
an accident: **writing a count before running it is the error I am personally prone to**, which is
precisely why the verifier and the agent-side re-run belong in the SOP.

**57. Declined: a chapter 5104 child-care consent clause.** The B11 agent asked whether to fetch
chapter 5104 and build a conditional-consent clause, noting the library already holds
`{{max_children_in_care}}` and `{{child_care_liability_amount}}`. Declined for this pass. The blocker
is real: **R.C. 5104.052's fire-safety rules for licensed type B homes could not be read**, so the
conditions a landlord would be consenting to cannot be stated, and a consent clause whose conditions
are blank is worse than no clause. The landlord's position is already secure — Ohio lets a lease
prohibit home child care outright, which `edu-family-child-care-oh` and
`edu-purpose-limitation-oh` both now say. Carried as a candidate once R.C. 5104.052 and 5104.01 are
read.

**58. Declined: a second condition-inspection education row.** B12 asked whether it should add
`edu-no-condition-inspection-rule-oh` alongside the `condition-report-oh` clause it wrote. No — the
absence is already stated by `edu-no-deposit-standards-disclosure-oh` and
`edu-no-deposit-cost-schedule-oh`, and rule 57 does not require a pair where the education half is
already carried elsewhere. The clause is the useful output, because R.C. 5321.16(B)'s "or the rental
agreement" limb is what makes a joint inspection evidentially worth doing.

**59. Two Ohio statutes the batches turned up that no other state's row will have.**
**R.C. 3767.29** forbids anyone to knowingly permit an abandoned refrigerator of at least 1.5 cubic
feet with an opening of at least 50 square inches and a securable door to remain on premises under
their control where children can reach it, without removing the hinges and latches — the only place
outside R.C. 5321.05(A)(7)'s misspelled "regrigerator" that the Revised Code names a refrigerator.
And **R.C. 3737.22(F) and (G)** have the fire marshal cooperate on the R.C. 5104.052 fire-safety rules
for *licensed* type B child care homes and actually **inspect** an *unlicensed* type B home against
them as a precondition of USDA food-program approval — which proves the R.C. 5104.052 regime exists
and reaches the landlord's house, even though its terms could not be read.

**60. A jurisdictional trap the B11 agent caught itself about to walk into, worth recording for every
state.** It nearly wrote that Ohio municipal courts have general jurisdiction over the recovery of
real property. **R.C. 1901.18(B)(3) is the Cleveland municipal court only** — division (B) opens "The
Cleveland municipal court also shall have jurisdiction". The general grant is **(A)(8)**, forcible
entry and detainer alone. A row written from (B) would have told every Ohio landlord outside
Cleveland that they had a forum they do not have.

### Decisions on points B13-ownership raised

**61. The pass's last two `NOT_LOCATED` verdicts are closed, and both answers reverse an assumption.**

- **`eminent-domain` → PRESENT.** The agent declined to guess section numbers beyond R.C. 163.01,
  which is why the fetch was targeted. **R.C. 163.01(E)** defines "owner" as anyone "having **any
  estate, title, or interest** in any real property sought to be appropriated", and (F) makes "real
  property" include "any estate, title, or interest" in it. A leasehold is an estate, so **a tenant is
  an "owner" in their own right** — entitled to the "good faith offer" an agency must make before
  commencing (R.C. 163.01(J), required by R.C. 163.04(B)), entitled to appear, and entitled to
  compensation for the leasehold. A landlord does not speak for the tenant and cannot settle the
  tenant's claim with their own. The row is bounded to the definition: the apportionment,
  deposit-and-possession and lease-effect sections were **not** read and are named, not described.
  This also confirms the agent was right **not** to tag `eminent-domain-vi`, which rests on "no
  statute changes this" — now known to be wrong for Ohio.
- **`construction-liens` → PRESENT.** This topic could not be answered from inside the corpus at all:
  `mechanic's lien`, `mechanics lien` and `materialman` all return **0**. **R.C. 1311.02** gives a lien
  to anyone working or supplying for an improvement "undertaken by virtue of a contract ... with the
  owner, part owner, **or lessee** of any interest in real estate", reaching "the improvement and all
  interests that the owner, part owner, or lessee may have ... in the land or **leasehold**". So a
  tenant's own contract **can** generate a lien, and what it reaches is expressed in terms of the
  contracting party's interest — a tenant's contractor is reaching the leasehold, not the fee. **I
  labelled that a reading and not a holding**, deliberately: the sentence lists all three capacities
  together, and the sections that would settle it (R.C. 1311.03, 1311.04's notice of commencement, the
  rest of 1311.04 to 1311.14, and 1311.55 to 1311.57 and 1311.71 to 1311.80, which
  R.C. 1309.109(D)(2)(a) names) were not read. The body's practical advice is written not to depend on
  the answer. The agent was also right not to tag `no-liens-ar`: its "will pay Landlord's reasonable
  costs" limb runs straight into R.C. 5321.13(C).

**62. I ran the library-wide sweep the agent suggested, and it found something.** Its fourth question
was that R.C. 5321.13(C) voids *any* agreement to pay the landlord's attorney's fees — in the lease or
any other landlord-tenant agreement — so any Ohio clause making a tenant pay the landlord's costs may
be exposed, and a sweep would beat per-clause fixes. I swept every `LEASE_CLAUSE` in the delta twice,
once for fee and cost language and once for indemnity-shaped language. **On fees the delta is clean:**
the only two clauses that mention attorney's fees do so to **disclaim** them, which is right.
**On indemnity the sweep found two worth acting on:**

- **`tenant-caused-fines-as`, the shared row this pass tagged for Ohio, carries the same defect as
  `hoa-compliance`** — and on reflection I think the B04 agent's tag note was too generous to it. The
  clause says "Tenant will **reimburse Landlord** the amount Landlord paid". A municipal fine is
  assessed on the **owner** by law, so it is a "liability of the landlord arising under law" within
  R.C. 5321.13(D) even where the tenant caused the condition, and "reimburse" is indemnity framing,
  which is what (D) looks at. **The tag still stands, because Ohio is better placed than the row's
  home jurisdiction:** R.C. 5321.05(C)(1) gives the landlord "any actual damages that result from the
  violation" of R.C. 5321.05, and a fine paid because the tenant breached (A)(5)'s code-compliance
  duty **is** such a damage. The money is recoverable — as statutory damages, not as an indemnity. The
  clause simply takes the indemnity route to it. I have put the damages wording I would use into the
  Ohio tag note and flagged that **Claude Code should look at this row and `hoa-compliance` together:
  they are the same problem in two clauses.**
- **`animal-care-access-oh`, a clause this pass wrote, used the same framing**, so I reframed it:
  "Tenant will reimburse Landlord for any boarding, veterinary or removal costs" became "Tenant will
  be liable to Landlord for ... as damages for breach of this Lease". Nothing of substance changes and
  the same money is recovered — those costs are **not** a liability of the landlord arising under law
  at all, since the landlord incurs them voluntarily caring for the tenant's animal — but there is no
  reason to use the shape that produced the `hoa-compliance` finding when damages framing says the
  same thing and is unambiguously safe.

**63. The agent found a limb of R.C. 5321.18 my own brief had missed, and it is the sharper half of
the section.** I told it R.C. 5321.18 prescribes "owner and agent name and address". It does — but for
an **entity** owner or agent the address "shall be the principal place of business in the county in
which the residential property is situated or if there is no place of business in such county then
its principal place of business in this state." **An out-of-state entity owner with no Ohio place of
business cannot satisfy that literally**, and R.C. 5321.18(C)'s sanction is that the landlord
**waives the right to the tenant's R.C. 5321.07 and 5321.08 notices**. That is now the centre of
`edu-nonresident-owner-agent-oh`, and it discharges `landlord-identity-oh`'s standing caveat that the
entity rules had been read only at chapter level.

**64. Three corrections of emphasis to `edu-nuisance-receivership-oh`, whose own flag is now
discharged.** Its notes said the R.C. 3767.41 procedure was unread; the agent read the section whole
(33,868 characters). The row is not wrong, but three things it omits change the picture: **(C)(2)** a
receiver cannot be appointed until the judge has **first offered mortgagees, lienholders and
interested parties, in order of priority, the chance to do the work** — the row jumps straight to
appointment; **(J)(2)** the judge may order a sale if the receivership's costs are unpaid three days
after a declaration of abatement; and **(K)** the purchaser's title is clear only of unsatisfiable
**state and local** tax liens, so other liens, a filed federal tax lien, and recorded easements and
covenants survive. And one the pass should carry everywhere: **(F)(1) lets the receiver evict
tenants** — Ohio gives a sitting residential tenant no protection from a receiver, which is the
opposite of the sibling-state pattern.

**65. `landlord-lien` is answered by an exclusion, which is a better answer than a prohibition.**
"Landlord's lien" returns exactly one section in the held corpus — **R.C. 1309.109(D)(1)**, which
**excludes** a landlord's lien from UCC Article 9. That is a carve-out, not a grant: Ohio confers no
statutory landlord's lien on a residential tenant's goods. And the apparent workaround fails:
R.C. 1309.609(B)(2)'s self-help repossession right is exactly what **R.C. 5321.15(B)** withdraws,
since no landlord may "seize the furnishings or possessions of a tenant ... for the purpose of
recovering rent payments, other than in accordance with an order issued by a court of competent
jurisdiction". With R.C. 2329.661(B) voiding every waiver of the exemption laws and
R.C. 2329.66(A)(3) naming a landlord as a depositary of up to $400 of exempt money, a consensual
security interest is not the route round it that it looks like.

**66. Three questions carried rather than answered, because they are the library's shape and not
Ohio's law.** The **Protecting Tenants at Foreclosure Act** is a genuine FEDERAL gap — Ohio's
foreclosure chapter is silent on tenants, so federal law is decisive, and the library appears to carry
only the CARES Act notice; the agent named the PTFA in `edu-foreclosure-oh` and expressly marked its
terms unreviewed rather than stating them, which is the right discipline. Whether the **builder should
ask for the county** and switch `edu-landlord-registration-oh` and the R.C. 5323.03 agent duty on and
off is a product decision, not a legal one — worth noting that R.C. 5323.04(B) makes compliance
"deemed to be in full compliance with any request by the state or any political subdivision" for
identical information, which is a real benefit to advertise. And the **residential-hotel scope
question** is live and unanswered: R.C. 5321.01(C)(3) excludes hotels "where circumstances indicate a
transient occupancy", but a chapter 3731 "residential hotel" is by definition non-transient (minimum
stay over thirty days), so a residential hotel looks like a hotel that is **not** excluded from
chapter 5321 — while R.C. 5323.01(A)/(E) excludes any R.C. 3731.01 hotel from registration outright.
Chapter 3731 **is** held, so this is answerable inside the corpus, and I have routed it to the
content batch's `scope` topic rather than leaving it to a later pass.

**67. The agents are now routinely catching more of their own errors than I catch of theirs.** This
batch self-corrected three battery counts written from expectation (`materials` 62→59, `distress`
1→2, `transfer of ownership` 3→11), inspected the hits in every case, and found that every underlying
claim survived — only the numbers were wrong. Across the last five batches the agents have caught
three, eight, three and three of their own counts. That is the practice I put in place after my own
error, working better one level down than it does at the top.

### Decisions on points B14-content and B15-ev-hoa raised

**68. The third error in my framing file, and the most consequential — it had propagated to 24
answers.** `OHIO_FRAMING.md` glossed R.C. 5321.01(C)(7) as the manufactured-home-park exclusion
"(now manufactured-home parks, ch. 4781)". **Chapter 3733 is Agricultural Labor Camps** —
R.C. 3733.41 to 3733.99, eleven sections, all held in the corpus — and (C)(7) excludes "Dwelling units
subject to Chapter 3733. of the Revised Code", so it is the **agricultural-labour-camp exclusion**.
The corpus README repeats the same error. The content-batch agent proved it has **always** been so
from the enrolled text of Am. Sub. H.B. 33 held in the corpus, which prints the limb with the old
words struck and the new inserted — "sections 3733.41 to 3733.49" becoming "Chapter 3733." — so it
pointed at the labour-camp sections before the amendment too. My parenthetical invented a renumbering
that never happened.

**Park lots are outside chapter 5321 for a different and better reason:** **R.C. 4781.01(W)** defines
a park's "residential premises" as "a lot located within a manufactured home park and the grounds,
areas, and facilities contained within the manufactured home park", while R.C. 5321.01(C) requires a
**dwelling unit** — so a park lot is not a dwelling unit at all, and R.C. 4781.36 to 4781.52 is a
complete parallel code. **Every conclusion survives; only the citation was wrong.** The framing file
is corrected, the agent corrected the five of its own files that had copied it, and I corrected the
four others, each carrying a note saying what was wrong and who caught it. Anything relying on the
park exclusion should now cite **R.C. 4781.01(W) with R.C. 5321.01(C)**, never (C)(7).

**69. And an elision of mine blocked a topic outright.** My entry for **R.C. 1335.05** in
`FETCHED_SECTIONS.md` used an ellipsis that dropped the **suretyship limb** — the one limb a lease
guaranty turns on. The B15 agent caught it, **declined to reconstruct the missing words from
recollection**, and held `guarantor-renewal` at NEEDS_REVIEW because of it. Exactly right. I refetched
the section whole: "No action shall be brought whereby to charge the defendant, **upon a special
promise, to answer for the debt, default, or miscarriage of another person** … unless the agreement
… is in writing and signed by the party to be charged therewith". A guaranty of a tenant's rent is
that promise, so it must be in writing and signed by the guarantor — and a guaranty covering more
than a year needs the signature twice over, via the same section's one-year limb. Note it bars the
**action**, not the agreement. **Row upgraded to VERIFIED.** On the fee question the agent could not
settle: R.C. 5321.13(C) reaches an agreement "in any rental agreement … or in any other agreement
between a landlord and tenant", and a guarantor is not a "tenant" under R.C. 5321.01(A), so (C)'s
words do not reach a landlord-guarantor agreement directly — **but a guaranty of the tenant's
obligations can deliver only what the tenant owes, and the tenant owes no fees, so the ban bites
completely through the guaranteed obligation.** A separate guarantor promise to pay the landlord's
fees sits outside the words; **no clause is offered**, because no Ohio authority tests it.

**70. Four more `NOT_LOCATED` verdicts closed, one of them a substantial tenant right nobody expected
to find in chapter 5311.** `conversion-notice` → **PRESENT**. The agent refused to guess a chapter
5311 section number and **cited my own wrong guess at R.C. 5301.254 as its reason** — the right lesson
drawn from the right example. Searching the chapter index found **R.C. 5311.25(G)**: on a condominium
conversion the developer must give each tenant **not less than 120 days' written notice**, and "during
which time **the tenant may not be evicted to accommodate or facilitate the sale of any unit** if the
tenant is not in default"; must offer each tenant an option to buy their own unit, exercisable within
**not less than 90 days**, at a price no higher than the next **180 days'** public price; the periods
may run concurrently and may be waived **only in writing**; and where two or more tenants share a
unit the option is joint. All three figures are the statute's. R.C. 5311.27 prices non-compliance: the
sale contract is voidable by the purchaser for fifteen days with full refund, damages have a **$500
per violation per purchaser** floor plus costs and fees, and the Attorney General may bring a class
action. The duty is written as the developer's, which matters less than it sounds — in a conversion
the landlord usually *is* the developer.

**71. The two zoning topics: resolved as bounded absences, with the bound stated narrowly and what
would close it named.** `accessory-dwelling-unit` and `single-family-zone-lease-limit` were
`NOT_LOCATED` because chapters 303, 519 and 713 are unheld. I searched **chapter 519's section index**
(township zoning): **no occurrence of "accessory"** at all, and nothing catchlined for an ADU, an
occupancy limit, a definition of "family" or unrelated occupants. Ohio's zoning chapters are
**enabling** statutes granting power to local government rather than setting a state rule, so this is
an absence of a kind that can be stated — and the rows say so while naming R.C. 303.02, 519.02 and
713.06 and admitting that only one chapter's **index** was searched, not its section bodies. If that
is too thin for the library's standard the rows say to downgrade them. Both rows carry the
R.C. 5321.19(A)/(B)(1) split — a city cannot rewrite lease terms, but its housing and building codes
survive — and the **R.C. 5104.054 caution** as the model for how an Ohio provision in this family
reads: "a residential use of property **for purposes of** … zoning" binds zoning authorities and
nobody else.

**72. The EV caution the occupancy batch raised is resolved, and the worry was misdirected.**
`edu-no-ev-charging-right-oh` does **not** rest on chapters 4928 or 5301. The B15 agent read its notes:
it was built from a **whole-code** search of codes.ohio.gov, which is a *broader* instrument than any
37-chapter battery, and it opened R.C. 4928.01 as a hit without relying on it. Its only defect is
**placement** — the body states a flat absence while the bound lives entirely in the notes, which is
the same thing the new `edu-no-solar-rights-oh` was written to avoid. Reported, not edited.

**73. The second `hoa-compliance` defect does not exist in any Ohio-carrying row — and the cleanest
fix already exists in the library.** The agent checked: no active clause carrying OH lets an
association act in its own name against a tenant. The rows that do describe direct association action
(`edu-hoa-rental-rules-ut`, `edu-condominium-leasing-mi`, `edu-association-leasing-rules-fl`,
`edu-hoa-wa`) are correct for their states and wrong for Ohio on both limbs. Logged as a
contamination risk rather than an error. **And the fix for the original defect is already written:**
the library overrides `hoa-compliance` for Michigan and Oregon, and **`hoa-compliance-or` uses the
exact wording Ohio needs** — reimbursement "as damages for that breach" rather than the fine being
the tenant's responsibility. The agent did not write `hoa-compliance-oh` because withholding the
shared clause from OH is a change to an existing row, which the fence bars. Correct, and this is now
the single most actionable item for Claude Code at sync: **the wording exists, it just needs pointing
at Ohio.**

**74. A flag that could have left a row materially understating Ohio law, now resolved on the text.**
The agent warned that **R.C. 4112.021** might reach rental applications, which would undercut
`edu-tenant-screening-oh`'s statement that Ohio barely regulates screening: (B)(1)(g) requires a
prescribed Ohio Civil Rights Commission notice on **every credit application** in a type size no
smaller than the form, and (B)(1)(f) requires written reasons for a rejection within thirty days of a
written request made within sixty. It could not settle whether a landlord is a "creditor". I read the
section whole. **(A)(1)'s "credit" is the right "to defer payment of a debt" — and rent payable in
advance defers nothing**, so an ordinary Ohio tenancy is outside the section. But the margin is more
interesting than the core: **a landlord who regularly takes rent in arrears, allows a security deposit
by instalments, or puts an arrears balance on a payment plan is arguably granting the right to defer
payment of a debt**, and if done regularly may be a creditor — with that notice due on every
application, written reasons due on every rejection, and (D) giving a private action with damages of
not less than $100 plus fees. The cross-connection matters: this pass's
`edu-no-deposit-installments-oh` records that Ohio does not *require* instalments, and a landlord who
offers them voluntarily is exactly the landlord this margin catches. Both core and margin are now in
the row, with the cautious course rather than a conclusion, since no Ohio case law decides it.

**75. Three structural facts from the content batch that explain a great deal of the pass.**
**No Ohio agency administers chapter 5321** — intersecting the corpus for "department" and "director"
returns zero chapter 5321 sections, and "agency" returns only R.C. 5321.01, 5321.02 and 5321.07, each
meaning a *governmental agency that finds a code violation*. That is the structural reason Ohio has no
truth-in-renting act, no rights guide, no prescribed lease form and no registry: there is no regulator
to produce one. **R.C. 5321.07(A)'s destination rule is a sleeper**: the tenant's repair notice "shall
be sent to the person or place where rent is normally paid", so the lease's rent destination is the
statutory destination for the notice that opens rent escrow, and R.C. 3767.41(B)(1)(b) uses the same
hook. **And Ohio does prescribe type sizes — just not in a lease:** R.C. 4781.51(D) (a park rental
agreement's rights paragraph, ten-point boldface) and R.C. 1345.74(A) (the lemon-law statement at
execution of a motor-vehicle lease, ten-point all capitals), alongside R.C. 1923.04(A) and now
R.C. 4112.021(B)(1)(g). All sit outside chapter 5321, so `edu-statutory-forms-oh` remains correct for
an ordinary tenancy — but no row should say "Ohio prescribes no type size" unqualified. All three are
now in the framing file.

**76. The residential-hotel question is answered as far as the text goes, and the answer is
uncomfortable.** The B13 agent raised it; the B14 agent resolved it. R.C. 5321.01(C)(3)'s hotel
exclusion is **qualified** by "where circumstances indicate a transient occupancy";
R.C. 3731.01(A)(6) makes "transient" thirty days or less and (A)(4) makes a **residential hotel**
non-transient by definition; (A)(1) excludes apartments and dormitories from "hotel"; **(B) says
chapter 3731 does not apply to structures in which all units are residential premises**; and
R.C. 3731.041 lets a licensed *transient* hotel keep a guest 270 days in 40 per cent of designated
rooms. **So a hotel licence does not buy an operator out of chapter 5321**, and a long-stay occupant
of a licensed hotel looks like a tenant entitled to a court eviction rather than a lockout. `scope` is
`NEEDS_REVIEW` on that limb with the cautious course in the body. The contrast is sharp:
R.C. 5323.01(A)/(E) excludes **any** R.C. 3731.01 hotel and a college dormitory from registration with
no transience qualifier — so the same building can be **inside** chapter 5321 and **outside** chapter
5323. If Steinoak has extended-stay inventory in Ohio, this needs real legal advice rather than a
library row, and the agent was right to say so.

**77. Housekeeping worth fixing for any future pass.** The scratchpad is shared between agents, and one
agent's `bat.py` was overwritten mid-batch by an earlier agent's file of the same name. It noticed,
moved its runner to a unique filename and kept `ohsearch.py` as the single source — no work was lost.
I have told the last batch to use a uniquely named helper. The standing briefing should say so.

### Decisions on points B16-misc raised — the last batch

**78. The pass's final two `NOT_LOCATED` verdicts are closed. All 257 topics are answered and no
`NOT_LOCATED` verdict survives anywhere in the delta.**

- **`rent-tax` → CONFIRMED_ABSENT, bounded.** The agent called Title 57 the highest-value fetch left
  and noted that 41 states state a conclusion Ohio could not. I searched **R.C. 5739.01's definition
  of "sale"**: its only limb touching occupancy of real property is '(2) All transactions by which
  **lodging by a hotel is or is to be furnished to transient guests**', and the only other mention of
  real property in the section is (K)'s definition of "premises". **A residential lease is not a
  taxable sale, so Ohio puts no tax on the rent a tenant pays.** What the landlord pays is real
  property tax — which R.C. 5321.10(A) lists among the "customary and usual costs of operating the
  premises as a rental unit" — and income tax, recoverable through rent under R.C. 5321.02(C) rather
  than billed to a tenant as a tax. The bound is real and in the body: only the definition section was
  searched, chapters 5741 and 718 were not read, and **a city lodging or short-term-rental excise is
  expressly not excluded**, R.C. 5321.19(A)'s preemption not obviously reaching a tax.
  **And it joins up with the residential-hotel finding on the tax side**: the limb turns on "transient
  guests", R.C. 3731.01(A)(6) makes transient thirty days or less and (A)(4) makes a residential hotel
  non-transient by definition — so the tax line and the chapter 5321 tenancy line fall in the **same
  place**. Two independent regimes, one boundary.
- **`farmland-hunting-rights` → PRESENT, and it is a tenant right most landlords would not expect.**
  The last sentence of **R.C. 1533.10(C)** — easy to miss at the end of a long paragraph about owners
  — reads: '**The tenant and children of the tenant, residing on lands in the state, may hunt on them
  without a hunting license.**' The row makes clear what it is and is not: a **licence exemption**, not
  a right against the landlord, so R.C. 5321.06 still lets the lease prohibit hunting — but "they
  would need a licence" is not a lever, so a landlord who wants it prohibited must say so. Two limits
  are stated: division (F) preserves the separate deer and turkey permit, wetlands stamp and fur-taker
  permit, **none of which was read**, so whether a tenant needs those is undetermined; and the
  exemption reaches the tenant and the tenant's children only, not guests. Note also that on many farm
  lettings R.C. 5321.01(C)(6) puts the tenancy outside chapter 5321 altogether, so the lease is doing
  all the work. The section is eff. 2025-09-30 and is the pass's **second composite section** under
  R.C. 1.52(B), after R.C. 3749.01.

**79. A second row of this pass's own was incomplete, and the canvass caught itself again.**
`edu-scope-oh`, written in the content batch, was titled "Two Section-Level Carve-Outs" and named only
R.C. 5321.07(C) and (D). **There are four**, and the two it missed are on the same student-tenant
question: **R.C. 5321.03(C)** ("This section does not apply to a dwelling unit occupied by a student
tenant") withdraws the landlord's possession grounds in that section, and **R.C. 1923.02(D)** goes
furthest — "**This chapter does not apply** to a student tenant as defined by division (H) of section
5321.01 of the Revised Code when the college or university proceeds to terminate a rental agreement
pursuant to section 5321.031" — withdrawing the **whole forcible entry and detainer chapter**. I read
both section-open and fixed the row, title and body. The 1923.02(D) point is the most consequential of
the four, because it removes the eviction chapter rather than one section of it: a university
terminating its own student tenant's agreement under R.C. 5321.031 proceeds under the
notice-and-hearing procedures the lease must specify, not under chapter 1923. **This is the second
time a later batch found a defect in an earlier batch's row** — the canvass checking itself, which is
worth more than any single finding.

**80. No fourth correction to the framing file.** The last batch checked it against the corpus and
found R.C. 5321.03's five grounds, R.C. 5321.01(C)'s ten exclusions, and (C)(6) and (C)(7) all now
reading as the corrected file states, confirming chapter 3733 from the chapter itself — eleven
sections, agricultural labor camps. One gloss it suggests adding, and I agree: **R.C. 1923.04(A)'s
three-day notice binds any party commencing an action, but the prescribed conspicuous paragraph binds
only "a notice given under this section by a landlord to recover residential premises"** — so it is
not required for a letting excluded from "residential premises", such as the (C)(6) farm residence.
The row's advice is to include it anyway, which is right.

**81. A row-id error in my own brief, caught again.** I told the batch the source-of-income row was
`edu-no-source-of-income-protection-oh`. It is **`edu-no-source-of-income-rule-oh`** ("No Ohio
Source-Of-Income Rule, With One Disability Exception"). The agent confirmed the substance
independently — R.C. 4112.02(H)(1) lists race, colour, religion, sex, military status, familial
status, ancestry, disability and national origin, with no source of income, and "source of income"
returns **0 sections** corpus-wide.

**82. Two additions to `edu-no-servicemember-lease-rule-oh` that strengthen it rather than undermine
it.** Ohio **did** write active-duty termination rights — for a **motor vehicle lease (R.C. 1349.02)**
and a **cellular phone contract (R.C. 1349.03)** — and **R.C. 1349.04(B)**'s list of sections for
expedited Attorney General handling names R.C. 1923.062, 4933.12 and 4933.121 but **no section of
chapter 5321**. So the General Assembly legislated servicemember terminations twice, in adjacent
chapters, and did not do it for a residential lease. The gap looks deliberate, which makes the row's
flat statement safer rather than riskier.

**83. Three library-shape questions I am carrying rather than answering, and one real product
question.** The agent proposes a single shared **FEDERAL: FCRA** row instead of state rows gesturing
at it — three rows in its batch do — and the same for one shared HUD/HAP row behind the three subsidy
topics, and suggests `tpa-sunset` is really a methodology row about how to tell whether the law you
rely on has changed. All three change the library's shape rather than Ohio's law and go to Proposed
topic questions. The product question is sharper and is for Taylor: **R.C. 1349.19's 45-day breach
clock makes a tenant portal's operator the "custodian" under division (C), owing the landlord
expeditious notice** — so a Steinoak-hosted portal is inside Ohio's breach-notification chain, and
the incident process is worth checking against it. And one legal question I have left hedged rather
than answered, because it needs a lawyer: whether a **veterans discount** is a distinction on a
protected ground, given R.C. 4112.01(A)(22) defines military status by R.C. 5923.05's "performance of
duty" — so a discharged veteran is not plainly covered — and only a metropolitan housing authority has
express authority to prefer veterans (R.C. 3735.42).

**84. The canvass is complete: 257 of 257 topics, 268 delta rows, 144 confirmed absences, 103
present, 6 topics answered by a tag alone, 4 already answered, zero `NOT_LOCATED`, no integrity
problems.** Two counts in an earlier draft of this line were wrong and the independent check caught
both. **Eight** existing rows carry OH in the delta, not six: `extended-absence-notice-ks` and
`owner-occupancy-termination-ar` are tagged on topics whose verdict is CONFIRMED_ABSENT, because on
each of those subjects Ohio has no statute (hence the education row and its battery) while the
existing shared clause, read in full, is lawful in Ohio under R.C. 5321.06 (hence the tag). And the
delta holds **37** lease clauses, not 32. The full reconciliation is now printed in the log's §2 so
the four numbers cannot be misread again. Sixteen `NOT_LOCATED`
verdicts were raised across the pass and all sixteen were closed by fetching the chapter the agent
named. The independent check (§5) follows.


### Questions put to Taylor in the chat

- `alarm-tampering-fee`: What default should {{alarm_tampering_fee}} carry? Ohio sets no figure and no cap, and R.C. 5321.14 unconscionability is the only ceiling, so anything materially above the cost of restoring the alarm is exposed. I would suggest leaving it blank for the landlord to fill rather than seeding a number the library would then have to defend.
- `binding-arbitration`: Binding arbitration: I have written education only and no Ohio clause, because R.C. 2711.01(B)(1) takes possession outside the Act and FAA preemption is unresolved. If you want an Ohio arbitration clause at all, it should be money-only with an express possession and emergency-relief carve-out and no fee allocation - say the word and I will draft it as a separate optional clause.
- `certificate-of-occupancy-disclosure`: A genuine Ohio scope question surfaced here that no row in the library answers, and it affects which chapter governs the tenancy. R.C. 5321.01(C)(3) puts outside 'residential premises' those 'tourist homes, hotels, motels, recreational vehicle parks, recreation camps, combined park-camps, temporary park-camps, and other similar facilities WHERE CIRCUMSTANCES INDICATE A TRANSIENT OCCUPANCY'. But a 'residential hotel' under R.C. 3731.01(A)(4) is by definition NON-transient - it offers dwelling units 'for a minimum stay of more than thirty days'. So a residential hotel looks like a ch. 3731 hotel that is NOT excluded from ch. 5321, meaning both regimes may apply at once; and R.C. 5323.01(A) and (E) go the other way, excluding any R.C. 3731.01 hotel from the rental-registration chapter outright. Do you want this researched as its own topic (furnished or extended-stay lettings in Ohio)? It would also bear on edu-seasonal-tenancy-oh and edu-furnishings-included-oh.
- `common-area-lighting`: The batch brief asks whether, in a two- or three-unit building, a written lease may make the tenant pay for common-area lights wired to the tenant's electric service, and only from the start of a new tenancy (the Massachusetts rule at 105 Mass. Code Regs. 410.300(F)). Ohio has no provision either way: R.C. 5321.06 permits lease terms not inconsistent with ch. 5321, but R.C. 5321.13(A) and (F) stop the landlord's R.C. 5321.04(A)(3)/(A)(4) duty from being shifted. I have written the row to allow the cost allocation while keeping the duty with the landlord. Do you want a separate LEASE_CLAUSE row offering that allocation for small buildings, or is the education row enough given no Ohio authority blesses it?
- `condition-inspection`: This row is a LEASE_CLAUSE rather than a third education row, on the reasoning that Ohio's absence is already stated twice in the deposit family. If you would rather every topic carry its own education row even where it repeats, say so and I will add `edu-no-condition-inspection-rule-oh` with the battery in the body.
- `confirmed-absences-habitability`: This row names eleven sibling Ohio rows, nine of them written in this batch. If any of them is renamed or merged before the batch lands, the list needs the same edit - it is the only row in the batch with hard dependencies on the others' ids.
- `confirmed-absences-misc`: This row names sixteen sibling Ohio rows written in this batch. If any id changes before the batch lands, the list needs the same edit - it and confirmed-absences-outside-title are the only rows in B16 with hard dependencies on the others' ids.
- `confirmed-absences-outside-title`: The chapter list in this row's notes is the pass's whole not-held inventory, and it will go stale the moment the lead fetches another chapter. It is worth deciding whether this row should name the chapters at all, or whether the body's subject-by-subject version is enough and the inventory should live in the pass's own files instead of in a customer-facing row.
- `deposit-cost-schedule`: R.C. 5321.16(B) lets the deposit be applied to damages from the tenant's noncompliance with 'the rental agreement', which is a genuine lease hook - but it hooks the STANDARD of breach, not the amount, so I did not write a clause here. Do you want a companion LEASE_CLAUSE under this topic_key that lists the landlord's own turnover standards (what counts as damage beyond ordinary wear) without pre-setting dollar figures? That would be CONSTRAINED_TERM and would give the landlord something usable; this education row alone leaves them nothing to paste.
- `deposit-last-month-rent`: Prepaid last month's rent: I have written the row to say it should be treated as security deposit money under R.C. 5321.01(E), with verification_status NEEDS_REVIEW, because only case law settles whether money held against the final month is held 'to secure performance'. The statutory text and R.C. 5321.13(A) both point that way. Do you want this hardened to VERIFIED on the statutory text alone, or should it stay NEEDS_REVIEW until someone reads Vardeman v. Llewellyn (1985) and its progeny? The same question governs the nonrefundable-fee, holding-deposit and fee-in-lieu rows in this batch.
- `designated-repairer`: This topic carries BOTH an education row and a LEASE_CLAUSE in the states that have it (for example edu-designated-repairer-mp plus designated-repairer-mp, and designated-repairer-as). Ohio has no clause in the family and the one-file-per-topic rule let me write only one row, so I wrote the education row that carries the findings and the battery. Do you want a companion OH clause? It would be safe here because Ohio gives the tenant no repair-and-deduct right, and the shape to copy is designated-repairer-mn, which already uses {{landlord_repair_contact}}. It would need two Ohio edits: drop any 'unless a court orders it' softening in favour of Ohio's own routes, and add that the clause does not displace the R.C. 5321.07(A) notice address.
- `energy-data-release`: As with utility-disconnection-notice-authorization, a companion LEASE_CLAUSE is available here and I did not write one (one row per topic): a term requiring the tenant to sign and maintain the serving utility's data-release authorization and to provide usage figures on request is SERVES_LANDLORD under rule 55 and permitted by R.C. 5321.06. Want it as 'energy-data-release-oh'?
- `eviction-process`: The batch brief says R.C. chs. 2505 and 2727 are in the corpus. They are not - `ohsearch.py ch 2505` and `ch 2727` both return 0 sections, and the corpus README does not list either. R.C. 1923.14(A) expressly depends on ch. 2505's stay-and-bond machinery, so the stay-pending-appeal limb of this topic and of `eviction-hardship-stay` cannot be closed without it.
- `eviction-service-party`: Ohio's address confidentiality program (R.C. 111.41 to 111.99) is proved to exist from inside the corpus via R.C. 1901.25(A)(2) but the chapter is not held. It will also bear on the B07-dv-protected batch. Worth fetching once for the whole pass rather than per topic.
- `family-child-care`: The library has variables {{max_children_in_care}} and {{child_care_liability_amount}} from another state's home-child-care clause. Ohio could carry a conditional-consent clause using them, but the conditions would be entirely the landlord's - R.C. 5104.052's fire-safety rules for licensed type B homes could not be read, so I cannot say what the premises must actually have. Do you want chapter 5104 fetched and an Ohio consent clause written, or is the education row enough?
- `foreclosure`: The Protecting Tenants at Foreclosure Act (12 U.S.C. 5220 note; revived by Pub. L. 115-174, § 304 in 2018) is the federal limb that gives a bona fide tenant the balance of the lease plus 90 days after a foreclosure sale, and the library does not appear to carry it anywhere - edu-cares-act-notice covers only the CARES Act 30-day notice. Ohio's state silence makes the federal rule decisive here. Do you want it researched and carried as a second shared FEDERAL row, the way the CARES Act one is? If so it belongs in every state, not just Ohio.
- `foreclosure-disclosure`: The library already has a {{foreclosure_contact}} variable (used once). Ohio requires no foreclosure disclosure, so no Ohio clause uses it. Should Ohio get an OPTIONAL disclosure clause anyway - a voluntary 'the property is subject to a pending foreclosure, and here is who to contact' term under R.C. 5321.06 - so an Ohio landlord who wants to disclose has drafted words, or is that outside the pass's remit?
- `foreign-ownership`: R.C. 5301.254 is a duty on an acquiring owner, not a lease term, and it is the kind of thing a landlord meets once when buying. Should it live in the ownership-transfer workflow rather than the lease library, the way edu-deposit-on-sale-oh's product note suggests for deposits on sale?
- `forfeiture-redemption`: Does Steinoak's product cover land installment contracts at all, or only leases? R.C. ch. 5313 is a real Ohio regime with hard cure periods and an exclusive-remedy trap, and R.C. 1923.02(A)(7) puts it in the eviction chapter - but if the product never touches land contracts, this row is reference material rather than operative guidance, and the same will be true for every state.
- `frozen-standard-incorporation`: R.C. 5321.04(A)(10)'s citation of the SCRA as '50 U.S.C. App. 501' is stale - the appendix was editorially reclassified to 50 U.S.C. 3901 et seq. Ohio's existing `edu-no-servicemember-lease-rule-oh` says Ohio has no servicemember lease termination, which is right about state law, but R.C. 5321.04(A)(10) does make SCRA compliance an Ohio landlord duty. Should a sentence about (A)(10) be added to that row, or does this frozen-citation row carry it? I did not edit the existing row (briefing rule 3).
- `habitability-waiver`: edu-habitability-waiver-oh and edu-one-way-delegation-oh both quote R.C. 5321.13(A) and (F). I kept them separate because the topics differ (can the duty be removed at all, versus which way delegation runs) and the framing file told me to point at the delegation row rather than restate it. If you would rather carry one row, the merge target is edu-one-way-delegation-oh and the content to fold in is the as-is/rent-reduction/separate-document trio and the sentence-level qualifier analysis.
- `hoa`: Ohio currently relies on the shared `hoa-compliance` clause, whose second sentence the pass has found conflicts with R.C. 5321.13(D). The library already holds state overrides of that clause - `hoa-compliance-mi` and `hoa-compliance-or` - and `hoa-compliance-or` uses exactly the safe wording Ohio needs (reimbursement 'as damages for that breach' rather than the fine being the tenant's responsibility). Do you want an `hoa-compliance-oh` clause minted under topic_key `hoa-compliance`, superseding the shared row for OH, on the Oregon pattern plus a sentence recording that the landlord gave the tenant the association's rules? I did not write it: `hoa-compliance` is a different topic_key from my batch's `hoa`, that topic already has an Ohio row, and withholding the shared clause from OH is a change to an existing row rather than an addition.
- `holding-deposit`: A holding-deposit receipt is pre-lease paperwork, so it cannot live in the lease and would not be a LEASE_CLAUSE under rule 55. Do you want a short standalone holding-deposit receipt form for Ohio as a separate library artefact (amount, unit, hold period, credited-to, refund trigger)? It is the one place in this batch where the landlord is wholly unprotected and nothing in the library would cover them.
- `holdover-rate`: Ohio's holdover rate lives inside holdover-oh rather than in a separate optional holdover-rate clause the way VI, AS and others structure it. Do you want Ohio restructured to match the VI split - a bare 'Holdover' clause plus an optional 'Agreed Daily Holdover Charge' - or is the combined Ohio clause the intended shape? I have not changed holdover-oh either way; this education row works with either structure.
- `jury-waiver`: Do you want a LEASE_CLAUSE jury-waiver term for Ohio at all? My reading is that it buys little - R.C. 1923.09(A) already tries an eviction to the judge unless a party demands a jury - and its enforceability rests on case law outside this corpus. I have written education only. Say the word if you want a conspicuous, mutual, separately-initialled clause drafted as a CONSTRAINED_TERM instead.
- `landlord-registration`: Chapter 5323 only bites in counties over 200,000 population. Should the builder ask the landlord for the county and switch this row on and off, rather than showing it to every Ohio landlord? The same county test also decides R.C. 5323.03's out-of-state agent duty.
- `lease-content-requirements`: R.C. 5321.031 gives a college or university letting to its own student tenant a genuine REQUIRED_DISCLOSURE: the written lease must specify the termination conditions and the institution's notice and hearing procedures. Do you want a clause row for that segment, or is college-owned student housing out of Steinoak's scope?
- `lease-type-size`: A side finding from this topic that is bigger than this topic, and I have flagged rather than resolved it. R.C. 4112.021 (read whole this session, eff. 2008-03-24, H.B. 372, 127th G.A.) makes it an unlawful discriminatory practice for a 'creditor' to do nine things, and two of them would be real duties on a landlord if the section reaches rental applications: (B)(1)(f) requires a written statement of the specific reasons for rejecting an application if the applicant asks in writing within sixty days, to be provided within thirty days of the request; and (B)(1)(g) requires a prescribed Ohio Civil Rights Commission notice on every credit application in a type size no smaller than that used throughout most of the form. 'Creditor' in (A)(2) is 'any person who regularly extends, renews, or continues credit' and 'credit' in (A)(1) is the right 'to defer payment of a debt'. Ohio rent is normally payable in advance, which points away from a landlord being a creditor, and no case law is in the corpus - so I did not state an answer either way. But `edu-tenant-screening-oh` says Ohio 'barely regulates screening', and if R.C. 4112.021 does reach rental applications that statement understates the position. Worth a look by someone who can read Ohio case law and OCRC guidance; the cautious course costs nothing, which is to give written reasons for a rejection when asked.
- `lockout-for-rent-delinquency`: This row and the existing `edu-self-help-eviction-ban-oh` sit close together. I kept them separate because the 24 states that carry `lockout-for-rent-delinquency` are answering the opt-in question, and Ohio's answer turns on R.C. 5321.13(A)'s non-waiver rule, which the existing row does not cite. If you would rather have one row, merge this into the existing one rather than the other way round - the existing row is older and widely cross-referenced.
- `maintenance-duty-shift`: One point I could not settle from the text, so the clause states the cautious course and the row is NEEDS_REVIEW. R.C. 5321.04(A)(4) makes the landlord maintain in good and safe working order 'all electrical, plumbing, sanitary, heating, ventilating, and air conditioning fixtures and appliances ... supplied or required to be supplied by the landlord', and R.C. 5321.13(A) makes that non-waivable. R.C. 5321.05(A)(7) lets a written lease require the tenant to maintain 'any range, regrigerator, washer, dryer, dishwasher, or other appliances supplied by the landlord'. A range and a dishwasher are plainly electrical or plumbing appliances, so the same object sits in both lists and the statutes do not resolve the conflict. I drafted narrowly: the tenant takes upkeep and misuse, the landlord keeps the (A)(4) duty and all age-and-wear failures, and the bracketed instruction excludes water heaters, furnaces and anything in the building systems. If you want the broader version used elsewhere in the library (tenant replaces a failed appliance), that needs Ohio case law on (A)(4) versus (A)(7), which I did not search and which the corpus does not hold.
- `meter-conservation-charge`: Worth a decision at library level, not just for this row: the R.C. 1345.01(A) fork (a landlord who marks up a resold utility service is either a public utility or a Consumer Sales Practices Act supplier) touches four rows in this batch. It needs R.C. 4905.03 and some Ohio case law to resolve, and I have deliberately left it as a caution everywhere rather than an answer. Do you want it chased, or left as a flagged caution?
- `notice-to-quit-waiver`: Can we get an Ohio opinion, or a case search, on ONE point: may a residential lease waive or shorten the R.C. 1923.04(A) three-day notice to leave? R.C. 5321.13(A)'s non-waiver rule is limited to 'this chapter' (5321) and R.C. ch. 1923 has no non-waiver clause, so on the statutes alone the answer looks like yes - which would be a genuinely unusual power for a landlord-facing library to carry. `edu-waiver-by-acceptance-oh` asserts the notice is jurisdictional and that parties cannot confer jurisdiction by contract, but its cases are all about ACCEPTANCE of rent after the notice, not a pre-dispute contractual waiver. I have written the cautious course (never try it) and set the row NEEDS_REVIEW rather than claim a prohibition the statutes do not contain.
- `pet-fees`: {"question": "The shared pet-policy clause (OH-tagged) says 'Tenant will pay Landlord a pet deposit, if applicable, and pet rent of {{pet_rent_amount}} per month' without saying that an Ohio pet deposit is security-deposit money. It is not wrong, but an Ohio landlord reading only that clause would not know the pet deposit has to be itemized and returned within thirty days under R.C. 5321.16(B) and earns interest once aggregated past the R.C. 5321.16(A) threshold. Do you want an Ohio-only pet-deposit clause saying so, or is the education row enough? I have not written one, because the existing clause is accurate as far as it goes and the briefing forbids editing an existing row."}
- `pool-safety`: R.C. 3749.01(F) draws its line at 'a dwelling housing no more than three families', which is a different threshold from the ones Ohio already uses elsewhere in the library - R.C. 5321.04(A)(5) (four or more dwelling units in the same structure, for waste receptacles) and the three-or-fewer-units escrow exemption in `repair-escrow-exemption-notice-oh`. Three or fewer families lines up with the escrow exemption and with R.C. 3781.06(C)(9)'s 'residential building'. Worth adding to `edu-portfolio-thresholds-oh`, which currently says Ohio has five different small-landlord thresholds - this would be a sixth. I did not edit it (briefing rule 3).
- `rent-reporting`: The pass has already logged that no state row carries FEDERAL: the FCRA. This row and tenant-confidential-information both point at it without stating it, which is the right discipline but leaves a hole: a landlord who reports rent has real federal duties and nothing in the library says what they are. A single federal row, shared across states, would serve better than twelve state rows each gesturing at it.
- `rental-inspection`: The batch brief asks whether the lease itself can authorise a housing inspector to enter, so the tenant must provide access without separate consent. Ohio has no 'if the rental agreement so provides' hook for this, and R.C. 5321.13(A) bars waiver of ch. 5321, so I wrote the education answer that the lease can carry the landlord's own inspection entry (with R.C. 5321.04(A)(8) notice) but cannot manufacture the inspector's independent authority. If you want a LEASE_CLAUSE for cities with mandatory interior inspection programmes, I would frame it as the tenant's duty to cooperate with Landlord's entry when a municipal inspection is scheduled, basis SERVES_LANDLORD - say the word and I will draft it.
- `scope`: Does Steinoak have extended-stay, furnished or 'residential hotel' inventory in Ohio? R.C. 5321.01(C)(3) excludes hotels only 'where circumstances indicate a transient occupancy', while R.C. 3731.01 defines a residential hotel as offering non-transient units for a minimum stay of more than thirty days and R.C. 3731.041 lets a licensed transient hotel keep a guest up to two hundred seventy days. On the text, a long-stay occupant of a licensed hotel looks like a Chapter 5321 tenant, which would mean a court eviction rather than a hotel lockout. No Ohio case law is in this library. If that inventory exists, this limb is worth real legal advice rather than a library row.
- `security-devices`: R.C. 3781.103 is a real Ohio security-device statute with a narrow reach (plan approval under R.C. 3791.04, not retrofit). Ohio's existing shared `keys` clause row covers key handling generally. Do you want a separate OH LEASE_CLAUSE for rekey-at-turnover and tenant-added-locks (basis SERVES_LANDLORD under R.C. 5321.06), or should that stay inside the shared `keys` clause?
- `sfr-occupancy-disclosure`: `sfr-occupancy-disclosure` is one of the few topic keys in this batch whose sibling-state subject I had to infer (the sub-bullet 'Relative font-size / first-page placement disclosure' points at a rent-cap/just-cause exemption notice, e.g. California's). The Ohio row answers both readings, but if the library's canonical subject is narrower, tell me which and I will tighten the body.
- `student-housing`: Does Steinoak have any college or university customers? student-housing-oh is the only clause in the Ohio set that no private landlord may use, and if no institution is on the platform it may be better held back than shipped with a warning.
- `subsidized-inspection-refusal`: All three subsidy topics in this batch end the same way: the rule is federal and the library does not hold it. If Steinoak has customers with subsidised tenancies, one FEDERAL row on the HAP contract and the inspection standards would do more than three state rows pointing at it. Flagging rather than deciding.
- `tax-escalation`: {"question": "Tax escalation is the one clause in B04 where Ohio's prohibited-terms list creates real exposure, and I made a drafting call rather than leave it open. R.C. 5321.13(D) recognises no tenant agreement to indemnify the landlord for 'any liability of the landlord arising under law', and property tax is exactly that. I drafted the pass-through as an adjustment of RENT measured by the tax rise, with an express sentence that it is not an indemnity, because R.C. 5321.10(A) calls real estate taxes a cost of operating a rental met out of rent and R.C. 5321.02(C) blesses increasing rent 'to reflect an increase in other costs of operation'. No Ohio case law is in the corpus to confirm a court would respect the distinction. Do you want this clause offered for Ohio at all, or would you rather Ohio landlords be pushed to the scheduled or index option in rent-escalation-oh with the tax rise priced in, which carries no R.C. 5321.13(D) question?"}
- `telecom-access`: The federal limb carries this row and the federal text was not readable this session. If you want the Ohio row to state 47 C.F.R. 1.4000's safety and historic-preservation exceptions, or the inside-wiring and exclusive-access rules, someone needs to read the current CFR - I have flagged it rather than paraphrasing from memory.
- `tenant-confidential-information`: R.C. 1349.19's forty-five-day breach clock is the one duty in this batch a Steinoak customer could breach through the product rather than through their own filing cabinet - the platform is the 'custodian' under division (C) and owes the landlord expeditious notice. Worth checking that Steinoak's own terms and incident process match it, separately from the lease library.
- `tenant-employee-housing`: Should there be a CLAUSE sibling here? The body tells the landlord to write the occupancy-ends-with-employment term, and nothing in Ohio supplies it - R.C. 5321.06 permits it and R.C. 5321.14 is the ceiling, which is exactly the SERVES_LANDLORD pattern this library uses elsewhere (for example holdover-oh). I did not write one because the topic's other states all answer with education rows and because the term interacts with the R.C. 1923.04 notice in ways I could not test; say the word and it is a short clause.
- `tenant-forward-proceedings`: MP carries both a tag of this clause and an education row (`edu-tenant-forward-proceedings-mp`). I have given Ohio the tag only, because the statutory answer is a plain double absence and the clause is the whole of the practical advice. Say the word if you want parity with MP and I will draft `edu-no-tenant-forward-proceedings-oh` from the same battery.
- `tenant-repair-remedies`: edu-habitability-escrow-oh's notes flag that R.C. 5321.09 and 5321.10 were never read section-open and that 'they are what a landlord actually needs to get the money out'. I read both section-open on 2026-10-30 and have the content (four release grounds, the sixty-day trial, the tenant's bad-faith liability, and the partial-release list covering mortgage, insurance, taxes, utilities and repairs). There is no topic_key in this pass for getting escrowed rent released. Do you want a new row for it, and under which topic_key - a new one such as escrow-release, or extending landlord-maintenance with a third Ohio row? I did not write it because it is outside my sixteen topics.
- `tpa-sunset`: This row is really a methodology row dressed as a state row - 'how to tell whether the Ohio law you are relying on has changed'. If the library wants that advice it probably belongs in one place rather than in twenty-five states' sunset rows; if it does not, this row can be cut to two sentences saying Ohio's chapter has no expiry.
- `unauthorized-occupant-removal`: R.C. 5303.08 to 5303.17 (the occupying-claimant regime) can make a landlord who wins an ejectment action pay the occupant for good-faith improvements before taking possession. The row names it as an unpriced risk. Do you want it fetched and given its own Ohio row? It is the only place in Ohio's two possession routes where winning can cost the owner money, and no other state's row in this topic has anything like it.
- `utility-allowance-cap`: I could not see the variable catalogue, so {{utility_allowance_amount}} may duplicate a name already used by utility-allowance-cap-co or utility-allowance-cap-vi. Please reconcile before import.
- `utility-deposit-return`: This row's load-bearing step is a drafting judgment, not a quotation: no Ohio provision says 'utility deposit', so I applied R.C. 5321.01(E)'s 'any deposit of money or property to secure performance' to a landlord-collected utility deposit and concluded R.C. 5321.16 governs it. I am confident in it and the row states it flatly, which is what a landlord needs. Say the word if you want it hedged to 'should be treated as'.
- `veterans-incentive`: The military-status point is the one thing in this row a landlord might act on wrongly in either direction - a well-meant veterans discount could be a military-status distinction, and I could not settle it from the text alone. Worth a lawyer's read before this row ships, and if the answer is that a preference is lawful it should be said positively rather than hedged.

**New `{{variables}}` this pass introduces — seven, across five rows.** Every other variable in the
delta reuses an existing library name. Checked by extracting every `{{...}}` in the delta and
differencing against the master CSV.

| variable | row | what it holds |
|---|---|---|
| `{{alarm_tampering_fee}}` | `alarm-tampering-fee-oh` | the landlord's per-alarm administrative charge. Ohio sets no figure and no cap; the clause now recites it as a reasonable estimate of inspection, attendance and re-testing cost. |
| `{{utility_allowance_amount}}` | `utility-allowance-cap-oh` | the landlord's monthly utility allowance. No Ohio statute supplies one — chapter 5321 was read whole and holds no allowance provision. |
| `{{rule_change_notice_days}}` | `rules-regulations-oh`, `edu-no-parking-rules-notice-oh` | days of notice before a house-rule change takes effect. Ohio prescribes no period for a rules change, so this is the landlord's own. |
| `{{institution_name}}`, `{{conduct_code_reference}}`, `{{hearing_procedures_reference}}` | `student-housing-oh` | the three identifiers R.C. 5321.031 makes a college or university letting to its own student tenant put in the agreement. |

Each is the landlord's or the builder's figure, not a statutory one, except the three
`student-housing-oh` identifiers, which the statute requires the agreement to carry.

**Four of the seven fit an existing naming family and two do not.** `{{alarm_tampering_fee}}` joins
twelve `_fee` names, `{{utility_allowance_amount}}` joins nine `_amount` names,
`{{rule_change_notice_days}}` joins seventeen `_days` names (cf. `{{early_termination_notice_days}}`),
and `{{institution_name}}` joins twelve `_name` names. But `{{conduct_code_reference}}` and
`{{hearing_procedures_reference}}` open a **`_reference` suffix the library has never used**: it has
only `{{myhazards_url}}` and `{{payment_portal_url}}` for anything resembling a pointer to an outside
document, and no variable anywhere containing conduct, handbook, policy or code. The two variables are
right in substance — R.C. 5321.031 requires a college or university letting to its own student tenant
to carry these in the agreement — but what to call a pointer to an institution's own document is a
library convention question, not an Ohio one, and it is raised under Proposed topic questions rather
than settled here. If `_url` is the intended family, these should be renamed before sync.


### §5 Independent check


Rule 80 as scaled for this pass: at most two rounds, one agent at a time, round 2 re-checking only
the rows edited after round 1, stopping early on a clean round. The checker had the delta, the
corpus, the search tool and the fetched-section transcripts, and had written none of the material.
Its brief told it to find errors, not to approve the pass, and listed what does **not** count as a
finding — a drafting judgment the row says is a judgment, a cautious course on a point the row says
is unsettled, style, or anything outside its scope.

#### Round 1 — nine findings, all worked through

The most serious was not a legal error but a **correction that had only partly landed**, and it is
worth recording as a failure mode rather than an incident. Mid-pass a batch agent proved from
H.B. 33's enrolled text that R.C. 5321.01(C)(7) is the **agricultural labour camp** exclusion, not
the manufactured-home-park exclusion that both `OHIO_FRAMING.md` and the corpus's own README called
it. The lead corrected the framing file and ran a substitution across the rows. The checker found
that substitution had matched five phrasings and **missed nineteen rows**, four of them in the body,
and that `edu-no-fee-transparency-rule-oh` now carried a correction note contradicted by its own
body — "the note landed; the body did not. The row now contradicts itself." A context-window sweep
(sentence splitting fails here, because `5321.01(C)(7)` contains periods) then found twenty
occurrences and corrected **fourteen rows**, each with a note saying the first pass had missed it.
Three residual hits were inspected and are correct as they stand. The underlying law is now stated
the same way everywhere: a park lot is outside chapter 5321 because **R.C. 4781.01(W)** makes a
park's "residential premises" the lot and the park's grounds while **R.C. 5321.01(C)** requires a
dwelling unit — which is a different and stronger reason than an enumerated exclusion. This is the
source of the SOP proposal that a corrective edit be swept for all occurrences and re-verified.

The remaining eight, in the order the checker ranked them:

- **An error in a clause the pass wrote.** `drug-activity-prohibition-oh` told the tenant that "if
Tenant breaches this Section, Ohio law requires Landlord to give Tenant a three-day notice ... and
that Landlord has no discretion to overlook it." **R.C. 5321.05(C)(2) is conjunctive** and its last
sentence makes the second limb a term of art: 'For purposes of this division, actual knowledge or
reasonable cause to believe as described in this division shall be determined in accordance with
division (A)(6)(a)(i) of section 1923.02 of the Revised Code.' That division supplies a completed
police chain — a warrant naming the tenant and particularly describing the premises and the
substances, proper execution with a substance found, and an officer then informing the landlord. So a
breach of a **lease clause** triggers nothing mandatory. Both the clause and
